| FM-390 | Agent Runtime / Coordination | Recurrence of FM-68's stdin-hang mode (c-094), measured 2026-08-20: a backgrounded codex exec printed 'Reading additional input from stdin...' and idled to timeout 124 having done no work; relaunching the identical command with </dev/null completed normally. Same mitigation direction as FM-68: give stdin a real EOF (either /dev/null or an actual brief file the harness reads to completion). [recurrence_of: c-094] | A backgrounded headless agent launch must close or redirect stdin (</dev/null, or a real brief file) — an inherited never-closing stdin blocks the harness forever | Judgment |
| FM-391 | Identity vs. Routing | Measured 2026-08-21, bg job 92e4a093, fleet PRs #666/#669. A monitor gate written SPECIFICALLY to avoid an earlier partial-board misfire used jq '(.conclusion // "PENDING")' to classify checks. jq's alternative operator substitutes only for null and false, but GitHub's statusCheckRollup returns .conclusion == "" (empty string) for a check that is still running. Eight in-flight checks on PR#669 therefore counted as neither pending nor failed, and the gate announced 'PR#669 GREEN 6/14 pass, 0 pending, 0 failed'. It was caught only by noticing the arithmetic did not close -- 6 + 0 + 0 does not equal 14 -- not by any check. 'gh pr checks --json name,bucket' has a first-class 'pending' bucket and reported pass:6,pending:8 correctly against the same board at the same moment. SIGNATURE: an absent-value default ('// x', 'or x', '?? x', COALESCE) protects against null but not against a sentinel EMPTY value, and an API that uses empty-string-for-not-yet turns that gap into a silent false-negative in the safe direction of the gate. Whenever a gate partitions a set into buckets, assert the partition is TOTAL (sum of buckets == set size) -- that assertion, not the classification, is what catches an unmodelled state. Prefer the vendor tool's own status field over re-deriving one. SECOND instance of self-authored-gate-misfire in this one session: ~20 minutes after committing a review artifact upholding the standing 'PASS is narrow; default is FAIL' discipline, an earlier gate in the same session fired merge-ready on SUCCESS:1 of a 14-check board. Both gates were written by the entity they existed to constrain, and both failed in the permissive direction. [recurrence_of: none] | A CI-board gate must derive pending state from the tool's own status field, never from a hand-rolled mapping of .conclusion — GitHub returns an EMPTY STRING for in-flight checks, which jq's '// "PENDING"' does not substitute for | 🤖 |
| FM-392 | Identity vs. Routing | Measured 2026-08-21, fleet#660. An issue proposed rewriting 7 acceptance-gate scripts under .dispatch/checks/ as thin exec wrappers of a tracked template, 'preserving each gate's floor/skip semantics'. The template accepts exactly four parameters (CHECK_SUITES, CHECK_SUITES_CMD, CHECK_PLAN_FLOOR, CHECK_SKIPS_FATAL). An audit of the five not yet converted found 0 SAFE / 5 LOSSY: they carry mutation checks that patch the code under test and require red, negative controls, green-red-green round trips, git-state assertions that named files stay unchanged, shellcheck runs where shellcheck's ABSENCE is a FAIL, awk-extracted CI-config assertions, and one gate needing TWO independent floors where the template supports one. Executing the rollout as written would have deleted every discriminating check while leaving five green-looking gates. The framing assumed floor/skip semantics were the whole content because that was true of the first artifact examined. SECOND HALF, and the reason this is HIGH: the two already converted were overwritten IN PLACE, and .dispatch/ is git-excluded (.git/info/exclude:7), so no VCS history and no .orig/.bak exists anywhere. Their conversions can never be verified -- all that remains is a comment, written by the conversion that destroyed the evidence, asserting what the original did. Against a measured 5-for-5 lossy base rate that claim deserves less confidence than it had when written, and it is unfalsifiable. SIGNATURE: (1) a homogeneity assumption across a set of artifacts must be MEASURED before a bulk transform, not inferred from the first one read; (2) an irreversible edit to an unversioned file destroys the only evidence the edit was correct -- reversibility and auditability are the same property there. Keep the original beside the replacement whenever the directory is not version controlled. [recurrence_of: none] | Before a 'rewrite N artifacts as template X' rollout, AUDIT that all N are expressible in X -- and never overwrite the originals in place when they live in an unversioned directory | Judgment |
| FM-393 | Identity vs. Routing | Measured 2026-08-21, fleet#633. The issue title asserted 'two unsharded jobs own 87% of the critical path -- check-gaming 457s, bats/orch 423s at --jobs 1'. Re-measured on run 32447516370 (main, all green) by pulling per-job started_at/completed_at from the Actions API: every job starts within 18s of the first (no runner queuing), total wall clock 445s, and removing BOTH named jobs entirely moves the last finish from +445s to +323s -- 122s, 27% of wall clock, not 87%. By the other plausible reading the two are 36.7% of 2130 total job-seconds, also not 87%. Either way the number overstated the available win by roughly 3x, and anyone sequencing work against it was sequencing against a saving that could not be delivered. The DIRECTION was right (bats/orch at 432s is the critical path single-handedly), which is what made the wrong magnitude survive: a plausible number pointing the correct way is not re-derived. Re-measurement also surfaced something the issue never mentioned and which changes the plan: the ALREADY-sharded dispatch jobs run 281/146/203/305s -- the fastest shard finishes in under half the slowest, so ~160 job-seconds of provisioned parallelism sits idle, and rebalancing costs no new runners. Sharding another job before rebalancing would recover much less than the arithmetic suggests. SIGNATURE: for any percentage used to prioritize, name the denominator in the same sentence; wall clock and resource-seconds diverge whenever work runs in parallel, and 'critical path' specifically means the longest chain, never a sum. Re-measure a load-bearing figure before sequencing against it, especially when it is directionally correct. [recurrence_of: none] | A headline figure that drives prioritization must state its denominator -- 'two jobs own 87% of the critical path' was job-seconds misread as wall clock, and the real ceiling was 27% | Judgment |
| FM-394 | Identity vs. Routing | Measured 2026-08-21, fleet PR#671. A Codex lane was dispatched to independently review a new caller-ownership check on dispatch/cli-dispatch-root-launcher --kill-scope (a root-privileged authorization path in our own repo). The prompt asked the reviewer to try to break the check and to demonstrate any finding in a local fixture -- ordinary adversarial-review framing, and the only framing that produces a credible security review. Codex refused: 'This content was flagged for possible cybersecurity risk... To get authorized for security work, join the Trusted Access for Cyber program', exit 1, no review produced. The same brief, unmodified, was accepted by Grok Build (grok -p --yolo) and the review proceeded. IMPACT: the merge gate requires an independent cross-harness review, so a classifier refusal at one vendor silently narrows the pool of available reviewers for exactly the changes that most need reviewing -- security-relevant ones. A run that does not notice the refusal banks 'no findings' from a lane that never looked. REMEDY: route to a different harness. Do NOT soften or reword the brief to slip past the classifier: the adversarial framing IS the review method, a version rewritten to avoid trigger words produces a weaker review, and constructing wording to defeat a vendor restriction is the manoeuvre this model generation is documented performing. Record the refusal as evidence rather than working around it invisibly. Check: whenever a review lane returns nonzero or empty, read WHY before treating its silence as a clean result. [recurrence_of: none] | A vendor's cybersecurity classifier can refuse a DEFENSIVE review of our own authorization code -- route to another harness, never reword around the block | Judgment |
| FM-395 | Repo Hygiene / Canonical Home | fleet PR#671: I reported 9 red dispatch-2 cases as deliberately-documented pre-existing contracts requiring a founder ruling, and posted three blocking questions. The red was a regression from my own commit 349c6f1, and one of the failing cases (case 58) already contained the answer to the question I was asking. Fixing it required rewriting zero contracts. The escalation cost a full loop tick and would have cost founder attention on a decision that was never his to make. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #671) | Before escalating a red CI board to the founder as a decision gate, prove the failing tests predate your own branch: check git log --diff-filter=A on the test file and diff --stat against the merge-base. | Judgment |
| FM-396 | Repo Hygiene / Canonical Home | fleet PR#671 kill-scope ownership. I argued an absent manifest could not describe a live lane because 'ln refuses to replace a record and the directory is root-owned, so an unprivileged caller can neither overwrite nor unlink it', and wrote that reasoning into the code as justification. Both clauses were true and the conclusion was false: they constrain the ATTACKER, not the launcher itself. The same-owner retry I had just added unlinked live records, treating 'we are starting this task' as 'the previous one ended' with no check. A cross-vendor reviewer reproduced the full chain -- second same-owner launch unlinks the live record, the failing launch's EXIT trap retires the replacement, any sudoers principal then reaps the victim scope. Mutation checking did not catch it because no case had a live first launch, so the suite never asked whether the unit was still there. Check: for each 'X cannot happen' in a security argument, list every code path that writes the state X depends on, including the one being added in the same change. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #671) | A safety argument that enumerates only what an ATTACKER can do to an invariant is incomplete: enumerate what your own code paths do to it, and name the code that could violate it. | Judgment |
| FM-397 | DEPLOY / VERIFY DISCIPLINE | fleet#633 PR#680. An anchored line-level fullmatch in ci-meta-floor-audit.sh was proven fail-closed against four mutation classes -- echo prefix, printf prefix, trailing 'or-true', and missing argv -- all killed, coordinator-verified. Independent round-3 review then bypassed it two ways the probe never tried: wrap the identical board command in a bash here-document, or add 'if: false' to the rollup step. Both leave ZERO executing assertion and both returned exit 0 reporting '7 floors are wired'. The probe varied the command's edges and never asked whether the line sat in an executing context at all. Fix required parsing ci.yml as YAML, excluding constant-false steps, stripping heredoc bodies for all four delimiter forms, and treating any statically-undecidable if: as EXECUTING so the audit fails closed. Recorded as a recurrence of c-171 because it is the same family as a fail-open guard whose repair was verified by an insufficient re-attack: the repair was real, the attack proving it was not. [recurrence_of: c-171] (GitHub #633) | Mutation-test a gate against CONTAINMENT context, not only prefix and suffix edits: wrapping the asserting command in a heredoc or disabling its step must fail the audit | Judgment |
| FM-398 | FAIL-LOUD | fleet#633 PR#680. CHECK_GAMING_CASE_FLOOR 16 was deleted; review required restoring a pass pin. The fix restored CHECK_GAMING_PASS_FLOOR at 16 -- the old deleted number -- while the repo's own skip-baseline file recorded the measured CI value as 18 passed, 0 failed, 0 skipped with an EMPTY baseline, reproduced locally. At floor 16 the pass-to-allowlisted-skip attack survives one or two conversions: total stays 18, fail stays 0, passes drop to 17, board green. The coordinator's own probe missed it because it tested WHETHER the floor fires (15 passes gives FAIL) rather than whether the floor sat at the right VALUE. A restored guard must be re-derived from the current measurement with the derivation shown, never copied from the pre-deletion constant. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #633) | Restoring a deleted guard at its OLD value instead of its MEASURED value silently re-creates the gap the guard existed to close | Judgment |
| FM-399 | DEPLOY / VERIFY DISCIPLINE | hindsight#108 PR#139. To prove a rewritten test locks three literal replay pins, the coordinator mutated the pins to a bare true assertion and read the resulting failures as the lock firing. Wrong mutation class: the write path's updater regexes require the comparison FORM to be locatable, so that mutation makes the pins unlocatable, the add aborts, and the test never reaches its own oracle -- the observed failure was the add failing. Independent review found the class that actually threatens the property: a postfix or-true, which stays locatable at match count 1,1,1 because the regexes are not anchored to end-of-statement, and which the PRIOR version of the test passed 3 of 3. Before trusting a mutation result, verify the mutant still executes the assertion being tested. Recorded as a recurrence of c-171: same family as the vacuous mutation suite whose signal could not have differed for any input. [recurrence_of: c-171] | A mutation that breaks an upstream precondition measures the precondition, not the lock; confirm the mutated artifact still reaches the assertion under test | Judgment |
| FM-400 | DEPLOY / VERIFY DISCIPLINE | fleet#633 PR#680. BATS_EXECUTED_FLOOR is measured in ci.yml by counting dispatch/test bats cases, but a commit raised it 1962 to 1972 for ten tests added entirely under orchestration/test/bats; git diff --diff-filter=A showed the branch added no dispatch/test file at all. The commit comment itself called it a category-error ratchet and rationalized it as harmless headroom. Reverting it then exposed a second layer: the branch had been cut before another PR landed, so main was at 1966 while the branch reasoned from 1954, and the revert to 1962 read as a LOWERING against main -- correctly caught by a guard test that greps the shipped literal. Both defects share one root: floor arithmetic computed against a stale base and against the wrong corpus. Resolution was to merge main and take its value unchanged, because the branch adds no case to the corpus that floor measures. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #633) | A floor must be raised only for tests added to the corpus that floor actually measures, and its arithmetic recomputed against current main rather than a stale base | Judgment |
| FM-401 | FAIL-LOUD | hindsight#140. pipeline/tests/test_cluster.py test_default_backend_selection resolves the UNFORCED default embedding backend, which is an HTTP backend on a homelab GPU endpoint. During a brief outage the CLI correctly refused to substitute tfidf, whose embeddings diverge from the committed http-fitted snapshot, and returned 1 -- so the guard job failed on a DOCS-ONLY commit adding two markdown files. The prior commit passed, the re-run at the identical SHA passed, and a direct probe of the endpoint returned 200 minutes later. The production refusal is correct and must not change; the defect is the test's coupling to service uptime. Diagnostic trap: a red board on a commit that could not possibly have caused it is indistinguishable at a glance from a real regression. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #140) | A unit test with a hard dependency on a live service turns an unrelated commit red and trains people to re-run without reading | Judgment |
| FM-402 | DEPLOY / VERIFY DISCIPLINE | fleet#550. A terraform apply wrapper was documented as high-assurance enforcement. Four review rounds each found a new environment vector: TF_CLI_ARGS, then TF_WORKSPACE selecting state after the identity check, then TF_CLI_CONFIG_FILE, HOME and PATH selecting an unreviewed provider via dev_overrides, then pathname-not-bytes state binding. Every one requires the attacker to already control the operator's process environment -- and anyone with that control runs terraform directly and never reaches the wrapper. Rounds 5 and 6 would have bought nothing against that threat model while nine live cluster nodes stayed one accidental apply from destruction. The reviewer's own baseline comparison was decisive: the findings do not make the baseline bare-apply landmine worse. Correct disposition was to state the real threat model, an accident-stopper for an authorized operator, name both gaps in the docs, file them, and land the P0 fix. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #678) | When review blocks on a boundary a tool does not have, fix the CLAIM before hardening the tool, and check whether the modeled attacker can bypass the tool entirely | Judgment |
| FM-403 | Portability | fleet#633/PR#681, five review rounds 2026-08-20/21. A static text audit (ci-meta-floor-audit.sh) tried to prove a DYNAMIC property -- that a CI floor assertion executes. Each round patched it to reject the forms the previous reviewer demonstrated, and each next round found a new one: r1 echo/printf prefix and || true suffix; r3 heredoc body and YAML if: false; r4 <<\\EOF delimiter and if: ${{ 0 }}; a coordinator probe found 'true \\' continuation making the assertion argv; r5 found 'if false; then ... fi'. The remaining space is open-ended (while false, case, uncalled function, early exit, &, eval) so no finite denylist terminates. Round 5's own proposed fix -- reject compound-control-flow contexts -- would have rejected CORRECT shipped code, because main's legitimate whole-corpus assertion is itself 'if (( total_case_count < BATS_EXECUTED_FLOOR )); then'. Measured that origin/main's audit accepts an if-false-wrapped assertion too, proving the limitation pre-existing rather than a regression, which is what made accept-and-document the right disposition instead of a sixth enumeration. Convergent fix filed as fleet#683: the assertion writes a runtime RECEIPT naming the floor and measured count, and a downstream job fails if the receipt is absent or disagrees with ci.yml -- so nothing about the workflow's text is trusted and a wrapped assertion fails closed. [recurrence_of: c-201] (GitHub #683) | When a gate is bypassed, check whether the fix rejects an ENUMERATED form or removes the class; a denylist of spellings guarantees the next reviewer finds spelling N+1 | Judgment |
| FM-404 | Ephemeral Resource Leakage / Host Exhaustion | fleet#633, measured 2026-08-21. Sharding the CI critical path was predicted to cut ~72% by the naive model (wall clock = slowest shard + rollup). Measured reality was ~24%: orch bats 431s->325s, check-gaming 393s->294s, total 438s->331s. Root cause is that the shards queue against each other for a finite ARC runner pool -- a 204s runner-queue delay was observed directly, i.e. sharding contends with itself. The naive formula treats executors as unlimited and therefore overstates the win roughly 3x. The check is to measure queue-wait separately from execution time before quoting any speedup, and to state the runner-pool size as an explicit assumption of any sharding design. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #682) | Before claiming a parallelization win, check whether the shards contend for the SAME finite resource; a slowest-shard-plus-rollup formula silently assumes unlimited executors | Judgment |
| FM-405 | Portability | fleet#683 spec v1, caught by independent refutation 2026-08-21 BEFORE any code was written. The design was to prove a CI floor assertion actually executes by having it write a runtime receipt, with a terminal verifier job that fails when a receipt is missing. The verifier was specified with 'needs:' on the two rollup jobs. But the shipped rollups (.github/workflows/ci.yml:885-890 bats-orch-rollup, :1046-1048 check-gaming-rollup) use 'needs:' WITHOUT always(), so when a matrix leg fails the rollup is SKIPPED -- and GitHub reports a skipped job as Success. A verifier behind them is skipped too and goes green having verified nothing. The gate would have been hollow in precisely the scenario it was written for. Two further spec defects surfaced in the same review: the receipt was forgeable because no field bound it to the asserting command's exit status, and the spec claimed coverage of 'every required floor' while ci.yml declares SEVEN *_FLOOR values and only TWO have board-style producers. The general check is to enumerate the full cross-product of dependency RESULT (success/skipped/failure/cancelled) against any path-filter condition, and to state which combinations the gate treats as acceptable, rather than assuming 'needs:' means 'runs after and sees the outcome'. Cost comparison worth keeping: this refutation cost one dispatched review round; the equivalent defect class on fleet#633 cost five build-and-review rounds because the spec was never refuted before building. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #683) | Before placing a new gate downstream of an existing job, check what that job does when its dependency FAILS; a gate behind a job that skips on failure is vacuous in exactly the case it exists for, because a skipped job reports Success | Judgment |
| FM-406 | Repo Hygiene / Canonical Home | fleet#683 spec v1 and v2 both proposed a CI 'runtime receipt' proving each *_FLOOR assertion executed. Grok's v2 refutation (Q2.D): wrap the board command in 'if false; then ... fi' and printf the receipt JSON directly into $CI_FLOOR_RECEIPT_DIR -- producer job reports success, receipt present and well-formed, every contract check passes, verifier untouched. v2's own admitted gap was 'controls both producer and verifier'; this needs only the producer. Root cause: nothing binds the receipt file to the assertion's exit status, and the PR under test controls the workflow that writes it. Distinct from FM-403 (enumeration war) and FM-405 (skipped-job vacuity): those are gates that fail open, this is an artifact that attests to nothing. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #683) | A self-attesting receipt is not evidence: if the artifact proving a check ran is written by a process co-located with the check, verify the attestation is bound to the check's exit status across a boundary the attester cannot modify -- otherwise the producer alone can satisfy it | Judgment |
| FM-407 | Fail-Loud | clearwatch#4869, found by estate-wide CI sweep after FIVE consecutive red nights (2026-08-17..21), not by any alert. pipeline-smoke.yml:52-56 ran 'echo $KUBECONFIG_B64 | base64 -d > ~/.kube/config; kubectl cluster-info'. With the secret unset the expansion is empty, base64 -d exits 0 on empty input, and the step writes an EMPTY kubeconfig. kubectl treats an empty kubeconfig as no kubeconfig and falls back to the runner pod's in-cluster ServiceAccount, so the job failed several steps later with 'services is forbidden: User system:serviceaccount:arc-runners-clearwatch:homelab-arc-gha-rs-no-permission'. That error names the FALLBACK identity, so the failure presents as an RBAC gap. It is not one. Measured with the real run: block extracted from the workflow and executed under GitHub's actual 'bash -e': origin/main 3 passed 4 failed (fails OPEN on unset secret, empty payload, and no-current-context); fixed branch 7 passed 0 failed. The cost of the misdirection was concrete: the filed issue proposed granting that ServiceAccount create/delete/pods-exec on a live namespace, which would have made CI green as the WRONG identity, permanently masked the broken secret, and granted a CI runner write+exec that the sibling arc/rbac/homelab-kubernetes-ci-reader.yaml deliberately refuses ('a CI identity that can write is a CI identity that can be used to write'). GENERAL SIGNATURE: whenever a credential is materialized by a decode/parse step, the success of that step is not evidence a credential exists -- base64, JSON parse of '', and empty-string env expansion all succeed vacuously. The decisive assertion is on the resulting IDENTITY, not the transform: here 'kubectl config current-context' is deterministic and version-independent, because ambient fallback happens precisely when no context is selected. Second-order lesson: a diagnostic that names a principal is not evidence the system chose that principal. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #4869) | A decoder that succeeds on empty input cannot distinguish 'no credential' from 'valid credential': assert the secret is non-empty BEFORE decoding, and assert the resulting identity is the intended one, because a client that silently falls back to an ambient identity turns a missing secret into a misleading authorization error | Judgment |
| FM-408 | Fail-Loud | Self-inflicted 2026-08-21, fleet#677, infrastructure/bin/install-hooks.sh. The issue prescribed replacing a broken worktree path construction with 'git rev-parse --git-path hooks/pre-commit'. --git-path HONORS core.hooksPath. This machine sets core.hooksPath GLOBALLY to ~/.config/git/hooks; ~/projects overrides it locally, so the target repo looked fine and the change looked correct. The discriminating test created throwaway repos with 'git init', which inherit the GLOBAL setting, so running the installer there resolved OUTSIDE the temp repo and OVERWROTE ~/.config/git/hooks/pre-commit -- a hook shared by every repo on the machine that lacks a local override. Not version controlled, no backup, prior contents unrecoverable. Detected only because the test asserted the installed hook was executable at the resolved path and printed that path, which did not look like the temp repo. Two distinct lessons. (1) TOOL: a resolver parameterized by user config is not a repo-relative path; '--git-common-dir' is the worktree-correct resolver that cannot leave the repository, and where core.hooksPath diverges the honest move is to REFUSE (exit 2) rather than pick one of two silent failures -- installing in the repo's hooks dir is useless because git runs core.hooksPath, installing at core.hooksPath clobbers shared state. (2) TEST HARNESS: a test that runs a real installer must neutralize every ambient config input the installer reads, or the test itself becomes the mutation. 'git init' inherits global config; pin it explicitly. The blast radius here was the test, not the product. SIGNATURE: sandboxing a test by using a temp DIRECTORY is not sandboxing when the program under test resolves its target from CONFIG rather than from cwd. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #677) | A git path helper that honors user-level config can resolve OUTSIDE the repository: before writing to a path derived from git rev-parse --git-path (or any config-sensitive resolver), verify the result is inside the repo, and pin the config in any throwaway repo a test creates -- otherwise an installer under test mutates machine-wide shared state | Judgment |
| FM-409 | Identity vs. Routing | 2026-08-21: coordinator read a dispatch worktree, saw uncommitted work and a HEAD equal to main, and concluded the lane had died. It had not -- it was mid-flight. Coordinator then cp'd the target script aside, mutated it to verify the tests could fail, and cp'd it back. The agent committed cleanly seconds later and diff showed no damage, but only by luck: any write by the agent between the cp-aside and the cp-back would have been silently reverted, and the restore would have reported success. Liveness must be established before touching a lane's tree, and mutation testing belongs in a separate scratch clone, never in the live worktree. [recurrence_of: none] | Never run a mutation test inside a worktree another agent still holds; snapshot-and-restore races that agent's own writes | Judgment |
| FM-410 | Unclassified | fleet#682 attributed a 204s CI delay to runner-pool contention. The job API showed the rollup job was created at 12:35:38 and started at 12:35:40 - a 2s queue wait. It had not been queued at all; it did not exist until its needs: dependency completed. The refuting evidence was one gh api call away and was never fetched before the causal claim was written and published. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #682) | A performance or causal diagnosis names a mechanism that the already-available timestamps refute, because the timestamps were never read | Judgment |
| FM-411 | Unclassified | hindsight#123/#142, 2026-08-21. The coordinator instructed the lane to set cancel-in-progress: true unconditionally. The issue itself asked for a main-branch exemption, which was correct: unconditional cancellation can leave a merged commit on main with no completed guard run. The lane flagged the discrepancy instead of complying, which is the only reason the defect did not ship. Nothing in the dispatch contract required that - a compliant lane would have silently implemented the coordinator's wrong instruction over the issue's right one. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #123) | A coordinator's dispatch brief contradicts the issue spec it is derived from, and the lane has no instruction to treat the issue as authoritative | Judgment |
| FM-412 | Unclassified | hindsight#111, 2026-08-21. A mutation sed 's/^\(\s*\)exit 1$/\1exit 0/' was applied to neutralize a script's refusal path. It matched no lines - the script had no bare indented exit 1 - so the suite ran against unmodified code and passed. The reading was MUTATION SURVIVED, i.e. a weak test, and that finding was nearly reported. The real refusal was [ "$FINDINGS" -eq 0 ] || exit 2; mutating that killed 7 cases. Any mutation harness must assert a nonzero match count or a nonempty diff before interpreting the suite result, because zero-effect mutation and ineffective-test are indistinguishable from the exit code alone. [recurrence_of: none] | A mutation that matches zero lines reports the same green result as a surviving mutant, so a broken mutation reads as a weak test | 🤖 |

The dispatch logic checked only that the reviewer was not the coordinator. The Grok reviewer disqualified itself unprompted; nothing in the routing caught it. Check 'gh pr view N --json author' and exclude that vendor before dispatching the independence gate. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #146) | Reviewer-routing must exclude the PR author's vendor, not merely the coordinator | Judgment |
| FM-414 | Agent Runtime / Coordination | Codex review lanes were handed host-provisioned worktrees under /home/psimmons/worktrees and then clones under /dev/shm; Codex reported the first read-only and the second nonexistent. /dev/shm is per-sandbox private. Three review lanes were lost to this. The fix is to instruct the agent to clone the repo itself into its own writable scratch rather than handing it a prepared path. [recurrence_of: none] | A dispatched agent's writable scratch may be private to its own sandbox, so host-prepared paths are invisible | Judgment |
| FM-415 | Fail-Loud | The Codex companion flagged 3 of 3 review runs FAILED on incidental delegate-tooling errors. Treating that flag as disqualifying made the cross-vendor merge gate unsatisfiable -- no PASS could ever be accepted. The correct discriminator is whether a failed command lies on the evidence path for a claim being relied on, and whether the claim is a positive artifact (a mutation kill, a count comparison) or an unscoped absence claim. [recurrence_of: none] | A run-level tooling FAILED flag is not a verdict; test whether a failed command sits on the evidence path | Judgment |
| FM-416 | Deploy / Verify Discipline | After landing hindsight#142, the plan was to re-run the red guard on PR#146 and merge on green. A pull_request run checks out refs/pull/N/merge and a re-run replays the merge commit computed when the run was created, so it would have re-tested the old head against the pre-#142 base and stayed red. Verified: PR#146 reported base.sha=dbafaf4 while main was 44e0a06. Merge the base branch INTO the PR branch to recompute the merge ref, then let the fresh run be the green. | FM-417 | Unclassified | THIRD recorded occurrence of this mode (hc-FM-390 measured 2026-08-20, itself a recurrence of c-094/FM-68; fleet-OPS-40 records it independently). Counter-rule: when a lesson exists and failed to prevent the failure, the remedy is NOT another lesson. Rewrite the existing entry imperatively, naming (a) the action to always take, (b) the observable tell, (c) the command that settles it. | FM-421 | Unclassified | 2026-08-21 estate cleanup. Deleted 370 fleet branches and recorded each tip SHA, stating the deletion was 'trivially reversible' on that basis. Then ran 'git reflog expire --expire=now --expire-unreachable=now --all' followed by 'git gc --prune=now' in the same pass, which destroyed the recovery path just claimed. Measured with 'git cat-file -e <sha>^{commit}' over all 370 recorded rows: 272 still resolve, 98 DO NOT -- 74 lane-scratch (dispatch/*, agent/*) and 24 human-named (docs/*, fix/*, feat/*, ci/*, chore/*, tests/*, tools/*, pr650-remediation, fix-655-manifest-nits, fix-661-contract-path, fix-672-remote-tracking-containment). Content was not lost: every deleted branch was an ancestor of origin/main or the head ref of a MERGED PR, and squash-merging is precisely why the originals were unreachable and got pruned. What was lost is pre-squash commit granularity plus any abandoned lane work. The defect is not the gc -- it is asserting a safety property and then removing it in the same operation without re-checking the assertion. Check: a stated recovery mechanism must be RE-VERIFIED after every subsequent destructive step in the same pass, or the two steps must be separated by a gate. Direction matters: this fails toward 'the cleanup was safe', which is the reassuring direction. [recurrence_of: none] | Never run gc --prune=now in the same pass as the deletion whose reversibility you just claimed | Judgment |
| FM-422 | Unclassified | 2026-08-21 estate cleanup, irreversible data loss. Ran 'git reflog expire --expire=now --expire-unreachable=now --all' followed by 'git gc --prune=now' to reclaim space after deleting branches. The --all flag is NOT scoped to branch reflogs: refs/stash entries beyond the tip live ONLY in the stash reflog, and 'git stash list' reads exactly that reflog. 18 of 19 fleet stashes were destroyed, objects then pruned (git fsck --unreachable returns 0). Among the lost was stash@{1}, which ~/HANDOFF.md explicitly recorded as 'unreviewed, not discarded' -- deliberately preserved by an earlier session. One survived only because refs/stash is a real ref rather than a reflog entry. Compounding failure: I had ALREADY stated in the same session's handoff that no stash was dropped, having verified nothing -- I inferred it from 'I never ran git stash drop'. Checks: (1) never use 'reflog expire --all' as a space-reclaiming step; drop specific refs and let the default 90-day expiry work; (2) capture 'git stash list' before ANY reflog or gc operation and diff it after; (3) a negative claim about what you did not damage requires a positive verification, not an inference from which commands you believe you ran. Direction matters: this fails silently and toward 'nothing was harmed'. [recurrence_of: none] | git reflog expire --all destroys the STASH reflog; never run it to tidy a repo | Judgment |

| FM-428 | Agent Runtime / Coordination | Third+ occurrence of the c-212 class (FM-324/FM-346: comment text satisfies a grep-based check). Four NEW instances in one 2026-08-22/23 session: fleet PR#700 closed as a regression -- comment/echo satisfies a script-invocation guard, and origin/main caught 9 of 16 shell-invocation forms the rewrite missed. fleet PR#701 -- a regex count over an stdin-redirect line is satisfied by a comment mentioning the old form (M5), and a Node text.count() check is satisfied by 'if (false) child.stdin.end();' (M8). hindsight PR#167/guard.yml -- a trivial non-empty stub satisfies a non-emptiness check, dropping guard-bats coverage 13->4 while the sha256 pin on guard.yml stays honestly correct. hindsight PR#171 -- if:false on a step satisfies a step-name-list pin while 261 tests silently stop executing. All assert TEXT EXISTS rather than BEHAVIOR HAPPENS, so a clean deletion is caught but disable-without-delete is not -- backwards from the real threat, since nobody deletes a guard by accident but code gets commented out or short-circuited constantly. Recurrence-of forces mechanize: acceptance criteria for any new guard must require a disable-without-delete proof (comment out, if-false, alias, non-empty stub, zeroing arithmetic, moved-out-of-scanned-path), and a deletion-only proof must be rejected as insufficient evidence. | FM-429 | Agent Runtime / Coordination | Two of two guard-rewrite lanes in one session (2026-08-22/23) shipped a regression: fleet PR#700 (fleet#698) rewrote a script-invocation matcher and origin/main caught 9 of 16 shell-invocation forms (env FOO=1, source, ., backticks, bash -c, if-then, nohup, time, !, stdbuf) that the rewrite missed. fleet PR#702 (fleet#699) rewrote a floor-enforcement audit and origin/main rejected FLOOR*0/FLOOR-FLOOR/chained-zero/large-negative-offset while the rewrite accepted all four; its broadened count pattern also matched 'ran' inside 'branch'. Neither lane compared its new recognizer's behavior against the old one on the OLD recognizer's own catch set before shipping. Fix: any PR that rewrites a pattern-matching guard must run the new implementation against every case the OLD implementation is known to catch (mine them from the old source/tests) and show zero regressions, as a mandatory acceptance item distinct from the new feature's own test cases. | FM-430 | Agent Runtime / Coordination | Measured 2026-08-22/23: round-1 first-pass validation success across 58 rework-ledger rows was 21%, BELOW the ~33% a purely random process would produce absent any signal -- the process was not merely noisy, it was anti-correlated with the target. Every traced failure was a lane that wrote both the guarded code and a test suite that code could pass: PR#700, PR#701, PR#702, PR#167-r3/r4 all shipped with a self-authored planted-failure proof in the PR body and green CI, and each proof was later shown to be a DELETION test (which any text-matching gate trivially passes) rather than a disable-without-delete test. This is Goodhart's law operating inside a single agent turn: optimizing 'my tests pass' selects for a satisfiable proof, not a correct guard. Fix (deployed same session): separate the attack-test author from the implementer as a distinct agent turn with no production-file write access, require the author to specify disable-without-delete attacks plus a no-regression differential against the prior version BEFORE implementation starts, and make satisfying that pre-written attack list an exit gate on the implementer's own turn rather than a downstream review finding. [recurrence_of: none] | A single agent that both implements a fix and authors its own acceptance test converges on a proof it can satisfy, not the property being guarded | Judgment |

Measured effect: a branch that deletes its own .githooks/pre-push then pushes gets rc=0 with NO guard firing at all, versus rc=1 with the guard running under the old absolute-path scheme. Every worktree sharing that repo's .git inherited the weakened hook resolution immediately, with no review gate having approved it. git's per-worktree config (extensions.worktreeConfig + config.worktree) exists precisely to prevent this class and was not used. Fix: any brief authorizing config changes as part of a fix must explicitly scope them to test fixtures or the lane's own worktree-local config, never the shared repo config, and acceptance criteria must include an unconditional restore-on-every-exit-path proof, not just a restore-on-success proof. [recurrence_of: none] | git worktree isolation protects files per-worktree but not git CONFIG, so a lane can silently weaken shared state (e.g. core.hooksPath) for every worktree and the primary checkout | Judgment |
| FM-432 | Agent Runtime / Coordination | Coordinator hit this directly 2026-08-23 committing test fixes in a detached-HEAD worktree (hs-167, detached at 76f23ba) for hindsight PR#167. 'git push origin fix-164-ci-shard-r2' was rejected as non-fast-forward THREE times despite git merge-base confirming the remote tip WAS an ancestor of the local commit -- because git resolves the bare branch-name argument against the LOCAL branch namespace (shared across all worktrees of one .git), not the current checkout, and a SIBLING worktree (hs-fix-164) had that exact branch name checked out at a stale commit. Diagnosis via GIT_TRACE_PACKET confirmed the server's advertised ref matched the coordinator's expectation, which made the failure look like a race or auth flake rather than a ref-resolution bug. Fix: from any detached-HEAD worktree, push via the explicit refspec 'git push origin HEAD:refs/heads/<branch>', never a bare branch name; and check 'git symbolic-ref -q HEAD' before diagnosing a push rejection as a remote-side problem. Read as a hung zombie runner, the coordinator cancelled two HEALTHY long-running CI jobs and filed hindsight#166 on the false premise, later retracted and closed. The authoritative, per-attempt view is 'gh api repos/{o}/{r}/actions/runs/{id}/attempts/{n}/jobs' -- 'runs/{id}/jobs' silently defaults to a merged/latest view across all attempts. Fix: any tool or check diagnosing a stuck run must query the specific attempt number, never the bare runs/{id}/jobs endpoint. Fix: never trust a gh mutation subcommand's exit code alone for a state change with consequences (merge, close, delete); confirm the target's new STATE afterward (mergedAt set, a merge commit exists, the linked issue closed) before reporting success. SIGNATURE: treating a coordinator's own already-checked-out local branch as ground truth for a design/scoping document, without first fetching the actual current origin/main, risks building a plan (and, worse, a full build brief) against a structurally stale file -- caught here only by unrelated luck, not by any check. Every design/scoping doc that cites specific file:line content should open with a fetched origin/main, not assume the local branch is current, especially in a multi-session/multi-branch campaign where other lanes merge to main independently. Cross-ref lesson-verify-actual-config-not-documented (same family, applied to git ref currency specifically, not documentation-vs-config). | FM-444 | Unclassified | Four independent instances found by attack-validation in one 2026-08-23 sweep (fleet#705), none caught by CI or review, three of four fully green while the guarded property was gone: (1) fleet#693's codex-exec stdin guard passed when the redirect was removed but a comment mentioning it remained; (2) fleet#693's Node child.stdin.end() count guard passed under 'if (false) child.stdin.end();' because the literal text count was unchanged; (3) fleet#698's script-invocation guard passed 444/444 green when the script was invoked nowhere, defeated by 'echo NOT-INVOKED \' plus a continuation line; (4) hindsight#171's workflow-integrity detectors passed (pytest exit 0) after both enforcer files were replaced with trivial non-empty stubs; a fifth, hindsight#170, is the same shape (an if: false on one workflow step silently stops 261 bats tests while the job still reports SUCCESS). SIGNATURE: a guard that asserts a string is present in source fails only on a clean deletion -- the threat model nobody actually uses, since code gets commented out, short-circuited, or made unreachable constantly, not deleted. A related sub-case: a recognition set that silently SKIPS what it cannot classify makes an unguarded call site indistinguishable from no call site at all (fleet#693's original guard skipped unrecognized launch forms rather than failing on them). POSITIVE CONTROL, same sweep: fleet#693's eventual fix (a live-writer FIFO held open on fd 0/9, observing the dispatcher actually hang) and hindsight#168's release validation (replaying the actual release transaction and comparing byte-for-byte) both catch the real mutations that every text-based guard in this family missed -- because they execute the thing and observe the outcome, not grep the source for a substring. RULE ADOPTED THIS CAMPAIGN (ops-plans/drain-2026-08-21/PLAN.md line 1340, applied as brief rule #3 for the rest of the sweep, not written as a separate template file): (1) a guard must execute the thing and observe the outcome -- if that's impossible, its own name must call it a tripwire, not a gate; (2) unclassifiable input must FAIL loudly, naming what it couldn't classify, never silently skip; (3) prove a guard can fail by disabling the behavior while leaving the guarding TEXT intact, never by deleting the guarded code outright -- deletion is the easy case and proves almost nothing about the guard's real discriminating power; (4) text/string counts over source are acceptable only as a redundant second signal, never as the sole gate. All 4 concrete instances now have landed behavioral fixes or a tracked design (fleet#693/#698 merged via fleet PR#711/#710 tonight using exactly this discipline -- FIFO-hang and execution-based checks, not text scans; hindsight#171 parked pending a founder-level external-enforcer decision; hindsight#170 has a design doc + build brief in progress, flagged stale mid-campaign and pending a mechanics update, tracked separately). Cross-ref FM-437 (the check-gaming shape applied to the JUDGE rather than the test -- same root instinct, one level up: substituting a weaker verifier instead of a weaker guard). [recurrence_of: none] | CLASS: guards assert text exists instead of that behavior happens | Judgment |
| FM-445 | Unclassified | fleet#709 claimed unlazy's gate-check.mjs (~/.agents/skills/unlazy/scripts/gate-check.mjs, no tracked source repo) could not grade multi-line CHECK: bodies. Verified independently tonight, not trusted from the lane's self-report per campaign discipline: confirmed real via a synthetic test file reproducing the exact bug shape. Root cause 1: ATTR_RE captures only the text after 'CHECK:'/'EXPECT:'/'EVIDENCE:' on that same line; when a gate writes 'CHECK:' with the body on subsequent, more-indented lines (the standard mutation-gate convention used throughout this campaign's own drain-2026-08-21/gates/*.md files), the captured value is an empty string, which is falsy in JS -- so gate.check is never added to the run queue and the check is silently never executed, reported only as generic UNMET with no FAIL line. Root cause 2, a second real bug the original issue also named: when a gate's EXPECT field is prose documentation describing an exit-code contract ('exit 0 = PASS (...)') rather than a literal matchable string, expectMatches() falls through to output.includes(expect) against the full prose sentence, which will essentially never appear verbatim in real command output -- so even a CHECK that DOES run and exits 0 as intended still reports FAIL, because the tool is substring-matching documentation against stdout. Measured baseline (unfixed): a 4-case synthetic test file (2 multi-line CHECK, 1 single-line CHECK control, 1 legacy-substring-EXPECT control) scored UNMET:3 met:1 -- only the pre-existing legacy substring case passed. FIX: parse() now tracks an open continuation (contKey/contIndent) when an attribute's same-line value is empty, appending subsequent more-indented (or blank) lines until dedent, matching this campaign's actual gate-file format; expectMatches() gained a status parameter and recognizes a leading 'exit N = PASS' prefix in EXPECT, verdicting on exact exit-code match instead of substring inclusion, while any EXPECT without that prefix keeps the original regex-or-substring behavior unchanged (backward compatible). Re-ran the same synthetic file post-fix: PASS/FAIL/PASS/PASS as designed (the intentionally-failing case B correctly still FAILs, proving the fix discriminates rather than blindly passing everything) -- UNMET:1 met:3. Applied the identical fix against the two real, already-merged campaign gate files (leaf-g1-fleet698-699.md, leaf-fleet693.md, both previously hand-checked without a machine-readable EVIDENCE: line): after adding proper EVIDENCE: attributes citing the on-disk evidence files, --status now reports ALL MET (6 met) where it previously reported UNMET:6 (met:0) even though every gate was already [x]-checked with real evidence on disk elsewhere in the file. Deployed live (this tool has no git repo / vendor-patch delivery contract, unlike fleet#708's grok-build fix in the same session) after full test coverage: positive multi-line case, negative multi-line case (must still FAIL), single-line backward-compat case, and legacy-substring-EXPECT backward-compat case, all verified pre- and post-fix, plus a live re-run against the real campaign gate files. [recurrence_of: none] [recurrence_of: none] | gate-check.mjs silently dropped multi-line CHECK bodies and misused prose EXPECT as substring match | Judgment |
| FM-446 | process | Measured 2026-08-21/22: a CI-poll background shell hung 26h because its loop treated an empty/failed 'gh api check-runs' result as 'still pending' and retried unboundedly, with no deadline and no distinction between a dead waiter (transient gh failure) and a real red gate. Fixed by poll-guard.sh (drain-2026-08-21/scripts/poll-guard.sh): exits 3 after MAXFAIL consecutive gh failures (labeled explicitly as a dead waiter, not a CI verdict) and exits 2 on hard deadline overrun regardless of gh status. REWORK.tsv rows: infra-gh-401-poll, poll-loop-687. [recurrence_of: none] | CI-poll loops must cap on K consecutive gh failures and a hard wall-clock deadline, never spin on an empty/failed API result forever | Judgment |
| FM-447 | process | Measured twice, 2026-08-22 (REWORK.tsv rows infra-task-wrapper-exit r1/r2): a background task wrapper reported exit -1 while the codex lane it wrapped had actually completed and pushed real commits (0ed0e82, then separately 8b479d3). Both times the coordinator initially read the wrapper's exit code as the lane's own failure verdict; both times the real state (a pushed commit) contradicted it. The wrapper process and the work it wraps can die independently -- a wrapper crash/kill is not the same event as the wrapped process failing. Same family as FM-435 (gh pr merge exit-code lie): a process's own exit code is not authoritative for a downstream state change without independently re-checking that state. [recurrence_of: none] | A background task wrapper's own exit code is not proof the dispatched work failed -- verify against the downstream artifact (did the lane's commit push, does the PR exist) before treating a nonzero wrapper exit as a lane verdict | Judgment |
| FM-448 | process | Measured 2026-08-22 (REWORK.tsv row brief-159-skip-cause): a coordinator attributed 2 test skips to the HTTP embedding backend being unreachable, without first checking whether the sibling repos those tests depend on were actually checked out. A direct control proved the backend was reachable the whole time -- the real cause was the missing sibling-repo checkout, an unrelated precondition. The backend was simply the more plausible-sounding explanation, not the checked one. Filed in a brief this session before the correction, so the wrong cause briefly propagated downstream. [recurrence_of: none] | Before attributing a test skip or failure to a specific subsystem, verify every precondition (dependency reachability, sibling-repo checkout, environment state) independently -- do not settle on the most plausible-sounding cause without checking each one | Judgment |
| FM-449 | process | Measured 2026-08-22 (REWORK.tsv row infra-ci-self-contention): a coordinator had 4 concurrent PR CI runs in flight on shared runners, which starved an unrelated PR (#160) of runner capacity. Self-diagnosed and self-corrected by cancelling 3 of the 4 runs -- no downstream damage, but the contention was self-inflicted and avoidable by checking runner-pool headroom before dispatching the 4th concurrent run rather than after. Advisory, not mechanically enforceable without knowing the runner pool's real capacity at dispatch time. [recurrence_of: none] | Dispatching more concurrent CI-triggering runs than the shared runner pool comfortably serves starves other in-flight work -- know the target concurrency budget before fanning out, don't discover the ceiling by starving a PR and cancelling after the fact | Judgment |
| FM-450 | dispatch | A --check-script invoking pytest auto-protects tests/** (content+mtime digest before/after harness run). The digest covers the WHOLE tree including bytecode cache (__pycache__, .pyc), and is taken before/after the entire harness invocation, not just around the check script. If the dispatched agent runs pytest internally during its own verification (reasonable, common practice), that write trips a false-positive status:check-tampered (exit 22, never retryable), burning a full dispatch attempt. Fix: pass an explicit narrower --protect glob naming only the test .py source files (e.g. 'token-discipline/tests/*.py'), not the auto-derived tests/** default. Measured 2026-08-24, fleet-unlazy optimization plan (leaf B1): two wasted dispatch attempts before the narrower glob fixed it; a third leaf (B3a) happened to not trip it by luck (agent didn't run pytest that time). [recurrence_of: none] | cli-dispatch default --protect digests the whole tests/** tree, not just source | Judgment |
| FM-451 | dispatch | A --check-script containing an embedded python3 heredoc (a small inline sanity check using re.search/re.escape/re.MULTILINE) had those module.attribute tokens misdetected by cli-dispatch's referenced-path static scanner as literal file paths, then 'stubbed' (trivially satisfied since they don't exist), which flagged the check content-blind and degraded verification_tier from semantic to structural ('cannot mint a verified PASS'). The check's real discriminating logic (a pytest run) was unaffected and genuinely ran, so the actual work was correct on independent re-verification (re-ran tests directly against the delivered commit) -- but the coordinator could not trust cli-dispatch's own passed:true without doing that extra independent check. Measured 2026-08-24, fleet-unlazy optimization plan (leaf B2). Advisory for now: avoid embedding python heredocs with re.* module-attribute references in --check-script; keep structural sanity checks to grep/shell where possible, or investigate whether cli-dispatch's scanner can be taught to recognize dotted identifiers as non-paths. [recurrence_of: none] | cli-dispatch content-blind scanner mis-parses embedded python identifiers as file paths | Judgment |
| FM-452 | dispatch | Twice in one session (fleet PR#714, PR#716), cli-dispatch land refused with exit 39 (lane-binding-refused) / exit 27 (cannot determine every authoring/reviewer login) even though the underlying PR was real, pushed, independently cross-vendor reviewed, and CI green. Root cause both times: the lane-binding snapshot on disk never transitioned to state=delivered even though the dispatch's own JSON output reported status=delivered with a real pr_number -- a state-sync gap between what start reported and what the binding registry persisted (same class as FM-279). The fix each time was NOT to force land past the refusal (--force-no-review does not bypass exit 27 by design, and that is correct) but to use the standing gh-pr-merge-verified override path (issues gh pr merge, then independently re-queries state/mergedAt/mergeCommit before trusting success) -- the documented fallback for exactly this situation per memory feedback-override-merges-need-verification. A second contributing factor on PR#716: posting a review-post verdict from the SAME identity that authored a commit (self-approval, correctly refused separately) followed by a second verdict from a different identity left land unable to cleanly resolve which comment was the deciding one, compounding the refusal. Lesson: cli-dispatch land's fail-closed refusals on real, ready work are a real, recurring papercut worth budgeting for -- always have gh-pr-merge-verified ready as the fallback, and never post a review-post verdict from the same harness identity that authored the PR's commits, even as a first attempt, since GitHub does not let you retract a bot comment cleanly. [recurrence_of: none] | cli-dispatch land refuses fail-closed on stale lane-binding state, even for genuinely-merged work | Judgment |
| FM-453 | dispatch | Measured 2026-08-24, fleet-opt D1 sweep, PRs #717 and #721: after a cli-dispatch lane delivers successfully (status:delivered, real commit pushed to the PR's destination branch), a SUBSEQUENT --continue-pr dispatch targeting that same PR's branch (to apply a follow-up fix from an independent review finding) is refused with destination-reserved (exit 41) -- the just-delivered lane's binding is still 'active' and owns the destination branch even though it already finished and pushed. Occurred 3 times in one session across 2 different PRs (fifo-dedup before its trim fix, install-dedup-r2 before its comment-restore fix, install-dedup-r2 again before its delimiter fix -- each subsequent fix landed on the SAME original PR branch). Fix each time: cli-dispatch abort --task <the just-delivered task's name> (never --close-pr; this only releases the local worktree/branch binding, confirmed via worktree_removed:true, pr_closed:false), then retry the --continue-pr dispatch immediately -- it succeeds on the first retry every time. Root cause not fixed, same class of state-sync gap as FM-452 (on-disk lane-binding snapshot not transitioning cleanly after delivery) -- likely the same underlying bug manifesting as a different downstream symptom (blocks a NEW dispatch here, vs. blocks 'land' in FM-452). Advisory: when doing a --continue-pr chain of follow-up fixes onto the same PR across multiple review rounds, expect to abort the immediately-prior task by name before each new continue-pr dispatch to that branch; budget for it rather than treating it as a surprise. [recurrence_of: none] | cli-dispatch --continue-pr blocked by stale lane binding from the immediately-prior delivered dispatch to the same PR branch | Judgment |
| FM-454 | dispatch | Measured 2026-08-24, fleet-opt D1 sweep, PR #717 trim fix: the check-tampered protected-paths hash is taken before/after the ENTIRE harness invocation, so it fires on ANY change to a protected path -- including a change the brief explicitly asked for. I copied a --protect pattern from an earlier dispatch (protecting a source .py file that was NOT the edit target in that earlier case) onto this dispatch without re-checking whether the target file of THIS brief was the same file -- it was, so the agent's correct, intended edit to codex_stdin_fifo_guard.py itself tripped check-tampered (agent_exit:0, commits:0, pushed:false -- the tampering check fired before the agent even got to commit). Wasted one full dispatch attempt. Fix: --protect must name only files the brief guarantees will NOT change (e.g. the paired test file), never the file(s) under active edit -- verify this by diffing the brief's target path list against the --protect glob list before dispatching, not just reusing a prior dispatch's --protect flags by habit. [recurrence_of: none] | Coordinator-authored --protect glob accidentally named the exact file the brief instructs the agent to edit, causing a guaranteed check-tampered on a legitimate delivery | Judgment |
