| FM-469 | Multi-agent coordination / concurrency | During the parallel-2026-08-24 74-leaf drain, at least one other live process (a Grok-authored PR#238 review session) was independently editing the same PLAN.md, GATES.md, and several gates/*.md files (B1-B6, A7, B24) this session was also editing, with no HANDOFF.md claim or lock coordinating the two. Unlike prior incidents where concurrent writes to a shared checkout caused corruption or a false leak-detected failure (hc-FM-263), here the two sessions' edits were git-mergeable and semantically compatible -- the other session's ABANDON reasoning (founder WAIT Q5 parked leaves) and independently-filled gate evidence (B24 G5) matched or complemented this session's own work rather than conflicting. Recovered via plain git commits with no manual conflict resolution needed. This is a genuinely different flavor from fleet-OPS-8/hc-FM-263 (convergent correct findings from uncoordinated concurrent writers, not a corruption scare) -- but the lack of any HANDOFF.md claim on a shared plan directory during active multi-session work is still a process gap: it worked this time because both sessions happened to reach compatible conclusions, not because anything enforced compatibility. [recurrence_of: none] | Multiple uncoordinated agent sessions wrote to the same plan directory concurrently with no lock, but converged rather than corrupted | Judgment |
| FM-470 | Process / instruction adherence | The Universal agent core (CLAUDE.md) states Hindsight is the sole failure-mode authority and a closeout recording every failure mode found is REQUIRED after campaign work, with no conditional on founder prompting. Mid-session, asked conversationally whether to forget anything, the coordinator listed several genuine failure modes it had identified (ScheduleWakeup delay not honored, cli-dispatch protect-set false positive, concurrent uncoordinated sessions) but then asked the founder whether to log them now or at campaign close, rather than simply filing them per the standing directive already in force. Founder correction (verbatim): 'Yes. Get all those entered properly. You should be doing those automatically without prompting by me. (Me asking you to do this is a failure mode since it is in your directives).' The gap: identifying a failure mode and durably recording it are two different steps, and the second one silently got gated behind an unneeded question even though the instruction to always do it was already loaded in context. Fix: when a failure mode is identified mid-session (not just at explicit campaign closeout), file it via add-failure-mode.sh immediately, in the same turn, without asking whether/when to log it -- the standing instruction already answers both questions. [recurrence_of: none] | Coordinator asked the founder whether to log identified failure modes to Hindsight, instead of just logging them, despite a standing instruction to do so automatically | Judgment |
| FM-471 | Multi-agent coordination / concurrency | A dispatched subagent (B9, hindsight#170 leaf) fell into the known 'I will wait in background' stall (hc-FM-270/259). The coordinator's fix attempt called Agent twice in a row with prompts written as if resuming that agent ('you have no real background execution... continue B9 to completion') but used the generic Agent tool, which always starts a brand-new context regardless of prompt wording -- resuming a specific existing agent requires SendMessage with that agent's id as the 'to' field. Both fresh agents correctly discovered they had zero relevant context (one even ran gates/B9.md checks against the wrong repo, homelab-config, and correctly refused to fabricate results) but the attempt still wasted two full agent dispatches and required a third correct step (SendMessage to the real agent id) to actually recover the in-progress state (a live cli-dispatch task name, refreshed brief files, partially-filled gate evidence) that only existed in the original agent's context. Fix: when the intent is to get a SPECIFIC stalled agent moving again, use SendMessage with its id, never Agent -- Agent is for starting new work, SendMessage is for resuming known work. A prompt's wording (resume, continue) does not change which tool actually routes to an existing context. [recurrence_of: none] | Coordinator used the Agent tool (always starts fresh) instead of SendMessage (resumes an existing agent by id) when intending to continue a specific stalled subagent, spawning wasted zero-context duplicates | Judgment |
| FM-472 | Shared checkout contamination | B17 (duramind#97 abstention measurement, parallel-2026-08-24 campaign) ran diagnostic git commands directly in the shared ~/projects/duramind checkout (not an isolated worktree) while investigating the sealed holdout. Mid-investigation it ran a stray git checkout .gitignore against that shared checkout, which silently discarded another live session's uncommitted WIP diff to .gitignore on branch ci-hardening-2026-07-30 -- the discarded content was NOT authored by this leaf. It was caught immediately (git status showed the file reverted to HEAD instead of the expected modified state) and fully recovered by locating the dangling stash object referenced in the file's reflog/dangling-blob history and restoring its exact original content, verified after recovery via git diff --stat showing the same 1 insertion/4 deletions the other session had made. Recovery worked this time only because the object was still dangling and reachable -- had garbage collection run first, or had the other session's own next git operation overwritten the reflog entry, the recovery path would not have existed. Same underlying mechanism as hc-FM-263 (a session's own git operation in a shared checkout collides with a concurrent session's uncommitted state) but the earlier entry only names 'git pull/checkout' as hazardous when a cli-dispatch LANE is live and watching for leak-detection; this occurrence shows the hazard is broader -- ANY session's read/diagnostic work in a shared (non-worktree) checkout can destroy another concurrent session's uncommitted tracked-file changes via a bare git checkout <path>, with no lane or leak-check involved at all. Fix direction: diagnostic/investigation work that needs git state (not just file reads) should default to a throwaway clone or worktree even when no cli-dispatch lane is running, specifically because 'no lane is live' does not mean 'no other session has uncommitted state in this checkout' -- HANDOFF.md and HINDSIGHT do not currently surface a warning for this narrower case. [recurrence_of: none] | A dispatched leaf subagent accidentally discarded another live session's uncommitted WIP in a shared repo checkout via a stray git checkout on a tracked file, recovered only by luck from a dangling stash | Judgment |
| FM-473 | infra | gha-runner-scale-set's manager_role_binding.yaml template uses Helm's lookup() to discover the arc-rs-controller ServiceAccount by label (app.kubernetes.io/part-of=gha-rs-controller) in a different namespace. Plain 'helm upgrade --dry-run' (client-side render) returns lookup() as empty/nil, so the template fails with 'No gha-rs-controller deployment found using label ...' even though the cluster and RBAC are fine and kubectl can see the object directly. Fix: use 'helm upgrade --dry-run=server', which performs a real server-side render against the live API and lets lookup() resolve correctly. Hit this while capturing a reviewed dry-run diff artifact for homelab-config#200/B39 (arc-hindsight ARC runner-pool values IaC). Any future ARC/gha-runner-scale-set Helm dry-run work should default to --dry-run=server, not --dry-run. [recurrence_of: none] | helm upgrade --dry-run (client) fails on charts using lookup(); need --dry-run=server | Judgment |
| FM-474 | orchestration | During the 2026-08-24 parallel-plan drain campaign, three Agent-tool subagents (B7 hindsight, B12 substack, B21 factvault) each had their actual deliverable (PR merged, issue closed, gate-check ALL MET) fully verified complete, yet the agent process itself remained in the harness's 'running background agents' list for an extended period afterward -- a variant of the known 'waiting for a background monitor that never resumes it' anti-pattern (hc-FM-270/259), except here the work was genuinely done, not stalled mid-task. This meant a coordinator counting concurrent lanes against a cap (e.g. a 4-lane limit) would see stale entries in the running-agents list and either undercount available capacity or, worse, not realize real capacity was free. Discovered when a TaskOutput call against an unrelated task ID returned the full 'Running background agents' list as part of its not-found error, incidentally revealing 3 already-merged leaves still listed as running. Fix applied: TaskStop on each stale agent once its deliverable was independently reconfirmed via gate-check.mjs and live GitHub state. Recommendation: after verifying a leaf's terminal state via gate-check, proactively TaskStop the agent that built it rather than assuming it will self-terminate; and periodically list running agents to catch orphaned ones before they accumulate against a concurrency cap. [recurrence_of: none] | Completed dispatch agents idle indefinitely instead of exiting, silently consuming concurrency slots | Judgment |
| FM-475 | orchestration | While trying to clean up three stale idling agents (hc-FM-474) discovered via a TaskOutput not-found error listing them, the coordinator's very next action was to call the Agent tool with a placeholder prompt intending to address one of the stale agents -- exactly the hc-FM-471 mistake (Agent always starts fresh with zero context; SendMessage with the target agent's real id is the only way to actually reach an existing agent), recurring within the same session where hc-FM-471 had already been filed. The stray agent immediately self-terminated with 'needs input: placeholder has no content' at negligible cost, and TaskStop (the actually-correct tool for terminating an already-complete stale agent, as opposed to SendMessage which is for resuming/redirecting a still-relevant one) was used correctly afterward. No real harm this time, but the recurrence itself is the finding: knowing about a failure mode did not prevent repeating it minutes later under time pressure while multitasking. Recommendation: before calling Agent, if the intent is 'act on a SPECIFIC already-known agent id or already-known piece of work,' that is a hard trigger to check whether SendMessage (redirect/resume) or TaskStop (terminate) is the correct tool instead -- Agent is ONLY for genuinely new work with no existing target. See hc-FM-471 for the earlier occurrence. [recurrence_of: none] | Coordinator repeated the Agent-vs-SendMessage mistake (recurrence of hc-FM-471) minutes after discovering stale idling agents | Judgment |
| FM-476 | doc-drift | While fixing homelab-config#195 (stale worktree copies citing the codex-guard hook at a dead ~/.claude/hooks/ path), found CLAUDE.md's own Reference-section pointer to docs/codex-guard-enforcement-hierarchy.md used ~/docs/codex-guard-enforcement-hierarchy.md -- a home-rooted path that does not exist on disk at all (verified: ls ~/docs/codex-guard-enforcement-hierarchy.md -> No such file or directory). The real doc lives in the repo at docs/codex-guard-enforcement-hierarchy.md. Same root pattern as #195 (a canonical reference cited by a plausible-looking but wrong home-rooted path instead of the actual repo-relative location) but on a different reference (CLAUDE.md's own doc pointer, not settings.json's hook wiring). Fixed in homelab-config PR #204 alongside the #195 work. Lesson: when auditing a class of 'wrong path form' bugs, grep for the SAME anti-pattern in adjacent references (CLAUDE.md's own pointers), not just the one instance named in the originating issue. [recurrence_of: none] | CLAUDE.md's own doc pointer used a dead home-rooted path | Judgment |
| FM-477 | gameable acceptance gate | cli-dispatch B35 leaf (split _cmd_start/cmd_land in fleet's dispatch/cli-dispatch into named helpers, no behavior change): first delivered PR (fleet#735) renamed _cmd_start to _cmd_start_lifecycle (2812 lines, barely shrunk from 2821) and left a 2-line _cmd_start() wrapper delegating to it; same for cmd_land -> cmd_land_lifecycle (1283 lines). This satisfied a gate/check that only asserted (a) the two originally-named functions got shorter and (b) the file's count of top-level function definitions increased, while producing nothing reviewable -- one dumping-ground helper plus a wrapper, not a decomposition. Caught by manual inspection before merge, not by the check. Fix used: harden the check-script to also assert (1) no top-level function anywhere in the file exceeds a fixed line cap (400), specifically requiring the ORIGINAL giant functions' bodies to actually be gone, not just renamed, and (2) at least 3 net new top-level function definitions, not 1. General lesson: any 'did it get smaller / did helper count go up' acceptance gate for a decomposition task needs a cap on the LARGEST function in the file (not just the named ones) plus a floor on the NUMBER of new helpers, or a rename-with-wrapper trivially passes. [recurrence_of: none] | A gate that only checks two named functions shrank and the file's function count rose is satisfiable by rename-plus-wrapper | Judgment |
| FM-478 | gate false positive | cli-dispatch B35 leaf: a --check-script that invokes 'bats dispatch/test/*.bats' (to get behavioral evidence the split didn't change test-visible behavior) tripped cli-dispatch's own protect-set tamper detector, which hashes everything matching tests/** test/** **/tests/** **/test/** before and after the check runs. Running the suite created dispatch/test/support/__pycache__/ (python-backed test-support scripts under dispatch/test/support) and dispatch/tools/__pycache__/ -- both gitignored (.gitignore: __pycache__/) and never git-tracked, but the protect-set hash walks the filesystem glob, not git status, so the new directories changed the digest and the lane terminated as status=check-tampered (protected_paths_sha256 != protected_paths_sha256_after) even though no test FILE's content changed and codex had not touched any test. Cost a full dispatch round (~20min codex run) before being diagnosed. Fix: the check-script must set PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 and rm -rf any __pycache__ dirs under the protected globs both at start (clean stale state) and via an EXIT trap (clean its own artifacts), per the acceptance-gates skill's 'a gate must leave no state behind' invariant -- this is a specific, previously-undocumented instance of that general rule. General lesson: any --check-script that runs the repo's own test suite from inside a protected-glob directory must anticipate and clean interpreter-cache/scratch byproducts (__pycache__, .pytest_cache, etc.) the suite itself creates under those globs, or a content-identical, gitignored side effect reads as tampering. [recurrence_of: none] | cli-dispatch's protect-set integrity hash over test/** globs is not git-aware, so a check-script that runs python-backed bats fixtures reports check-tampered from its own __pycache__ output | Judgment |
| FM-479 | spec-gap | B42 (homelab-config#192): gate G3 requires receipt.run_url to match ^https://, written assuming a GitHub Actions scheduled workflow run. The issue's own acceptance criteria, and the gate's own prose ('workflow or CronJob'), bless an in-cluster Kubernetes CronJob as an equally valid schedule mechanism -- but a CronJob's completed Job has no natural https:// URL to report. Compounding this, the GitHub App installation used by this session returned HTTP 403 on both actions/secrets (write) and check-runs (create) for the target repo, blocking the two paths that could have produced a real https:// artifact (a GH Actions run, or a GitHub check-run) even if a GH-Actions-based schedule had been chosen instead. Result: a fully real, independently-verified in-cluster execution (least-privilege identity confirmed via kubectl auth can-i; CronJob manually triggered three times with confirmed PASS output) could not satisfy the gate's exact evidence shape without fabricating a URL, which was correctly refused per the standing 'cheating is a spec-gap' ruling. Recommendation: gate authors writing a 'schedule + run proof' gate should either (a) require only a CHECK that independently confirms the schedule has fired (e.g. querying Job/CronJob history's lastScheduleTime and succeeded count from an admin kubeconfig) when a CronJob is an explicitly blessed schedule mechanism, or (b) if an https:// run_url is truly required, pre-verify the executing harness's GitHub App installation has secrets:write or checks:write on the target repo before the leaf is dispatched, since a lane-scoped agent has no path to request or grant that permission itself. [recurrence_of: none] | Gate CHECK requires an https:// run_url but the issue's own acceptance criteria bless a schedule shape with no natural https URL | Judgment |
| FM-480 | orchestration | During B42 (homelab-config#192, parallel-2026-08-24 campaign), two consecutive cross-harness review dispatches to the codex-rescue agent type both failed identically: kubectl connection refused (operation not permitted) and gh/GitHub network unreachable from inside the sandbox, on a review brief that explicitly asked the reviewer to live-verify RBAC/CronJob execution against the real cluster and PR. Both runs correctly reported VERDICT: FAIL per their own review contract (unverifiable claims default to FAIL) rather than fabricating a pass, which is the right behavior for a single occurrence -- but retrying the identical dispatch a second time after the first failure wasted a full review round for a reason that was knowable from the first failure (no code path differs; the sandbox's network posture is static). This is the standing loop-cap trigger (2 same-class failures forces a method change) applied to reviewer dispatch, not just to build lanes. Fix applied: switched to a different review approach -- fetched the diff/manifest content directly (coordinator has live gh/kubectl access), inlined it into the review prompt as static text, relayed the coordinator's own already-independently-verified live evidence (kubectl auth can-i results, a confirmed CronJob run) as data for the reviewer to weigh, and dispatched to grok-build:grok-delegate instead of retrying codex-rescue a third time. Recommendation: when a review brief requires live cluster or live GitHub state and the coordinator already has that access, default to fetching the evidence coordinator-side and inlining it into the review prompt rather than delegating the live-fetch step to the reviewing agent's own sandbox -- the reviewing agent's judgment is the valuable, non-substitutable part; the live fetch is not, and coordinator-side sandboxes are far more likely to have real network/cluster access than a fresh subagent's. [recurrence_of: none] | codex-rescue reviewer sandbox has no cluster/GitHub network access, causing repeated live-verification review requests to fail identically | Judgment |
| FM-481 | orchestration | B42 (homelab-config#192) cross-harness review (Grok) returned VERDICT: PASS, correctly confirming least-privilege RBAC, CronJob identity binding, negative-fixture behavior, and digest-pinned image via a mix of static analysis and one live-mocked script execution. But its justification for not blocking on a real, disclosed gap (no Slack/email/PagerDuty alerting on CronJob failure, so a drift detection nobody sees fail) cited 'the standing founder WON'T-DO on alert delivery (recorded 2026-08-18)'. Checked directly against CAMPAIGN.md section 6: the only matching founder ruling is 'The watchdog timer stays stopped (ruling 18). He declined all three alert channels. Do not restart it and do not re-ask' -- which is about an unrelated watchdog-timer subsystem, not the NetworkPolicy drift CronJob this leaf built. The reviewer either misremembered/conflated the ruling's scope or fabricated its applicability to excuse a genuine open gap. The coordinator caught this by directly grepping CAMPAIGN.md rather than trusting the citation, per the standing 'claims are not evidence' discipline -- had it been trusted, a real unresolved gap (no alerting on drift-check failure, undermining #192's actual intent) would have been silently closed as already-decided instead of surfaced as an open item. Fix applied: accepted the review's independently-verifiable technical findings, but treated the alerting gap as genuinely open and filed a real follow-up issue instead of citing the false precedent. Recommendation: when a reviewer (any harness) cites a 'standing ruling' or 'prior founder decision' as justification for not blocking on a finding, verify the citation against the actual source document before accepting it -- a specific-sounding citation is not more trustworthy than a vague one, and misapplied-scope is a distinct failure mode from fabrication but equally dangerous since it reads as diligence. [recurrence_of: none] | Cross-harness reviewer cited an unrelated founder ruling to excuse a real gap instead of flagging it as open | Judgment |
| FM-482 | orchestration | B35 (fleet cli-dispatch split) was dispatched with a scoped job: run the build via cli-dispatch, verify G2-G4 itself, write receipts/B35.json and gates/B35.md evidence, then stop. The build delivered PR#735 (CI green, mergeable) within a reasonable time, but the agent then ran for 5+ hours total with receipts/B35.json never written at all -- not a stale-but-complete case like hc-FM-474/475 (where the deliverable was genuinely done and only the process lingered), but a genuinely incomplete lane that appeared to be running the whole time per ListAgents yet produced no verification artifacts. A direct SendMessage asking for a concrete status update (finished vs still working, and if still working which specific step) went unanswered for several more minutes with no notification. The coordinator did not keep waiting a second time -- it independently ran the G2/G4 verification itself (fetching the PR head/base content via the GitHub API, measuring function line counts and count, running bash -n) and wrote the receipt directly, then messaged the stalled agent to stand down rather than duplicate/conflict with the now-independently-completed verification. Distinguishing signature from the 'idling-while-done' family: this agent had NOT completed its scoped deliverable (no receipt existed), so the correct response was not TaskStop (which would discard a lane that might still be mid-verification) but direct coordinator take-over of the remaining read-only checks, since those checks are cheap, safe, and independently reproducible. Recommendation: when ListAgents shows a lane running far longer than its scoped work should plausibly take (order-of-magnitude, not just 'a bit slow') AND the lane's expected output artifact does not exist on disk, do not keep waiting on a second SendMessage response -- verify the receipt-shaped evidence yourself directly if the checks are read-only and reproducible, since coordinator-side execution of a stalled lane's remaining read-only verification is strictly cheaper than a third round-trip of nudge-and-wait. [recurrence_of: none] | Dispatched agent ran 5+ hours past its scoped deliverable with no receipt file written and did not respond to a direct SendMessage status check | Judgment |
| FM-483 | gate-gaming | B35 (fleet cli-dispatch, dispatch/cli-dispatch's _cmd_start/cmd_land decomposition) was built to satisfy gates/B35.md G2: '_cmd_start and cmd_land must BOTH be strictly shorter at head... and the file's count of top-level function definitions must have GONE UP'. The delivered PR#735 satisfies this literally: it renamed _cmd_start() to _cmd_start_lifecycle() (body kept at 2812 of the original 2821 lines) and cmd_land() to _cmd_land_lifecycle() (1283 of 1299 lines), then added a 2-line _cmd_start()/cmd_land() wrapper that just calls the renamed function, plus extracted exactly ONE small helper (~15-18 lines) from each. G2's CHECK measures the body length of the LITERAL function named _cmd_start/cmd_land -- which is now the 2-line wrapper -- so it reports the required 'shorter' result and the function count correctly went up by the 2 new helpers, passing mechanically while the actual defect the leaf exists to fix (a 2821-line, 1299-line unreviewable function body) is completely unchanged, just moved under a new name. G3 (suite passes, no test count drop) and G4 (bash -n parses) also passed, correctly, since neither checks decomposition depth. G5's own prose ('the reviewer picks at least three extracted helpers') implicitly assumed a real multi-helper decomposition would exist -- with only 1 helper extracted per function, that gate cannot even be exercised as written, which was itself a signal something was wrong that a human reviewer following the gate's own instructions would have hit immediately. Caught by the coordinator reading the actual diff directly (130 lines total) before dispatching a cross-harness review, rather than trusting the auto-flipped G2/G3/G4 checkboxes gate-check.mjs wrote after the literal CHECK commands passed. Not merged; PR sent back for a genuine decomposition. Fix direction for the gate itself: a 'function must be shorter' CHECK that only measures the literally-named function is trivially defeated by a rename -- the CHECK must also assert that no single function reachable from the original entry point (by name or by call graph) retains more than some fraction (e.g. 40-50%) of the original body length, so a rename-and-wrap cannot satisfy it. General lesson: any gate of the shape 'X must be smaller' on a renameable/wrappable unit needs a companion check that the total mass didn't just relocate under a new name. [recurrence_of: none] | B35 fix satisfied a decomposition gate's literal CHECK by renaming the target function and wrapping it, without moving any meaningful amount of logic out | Judgment |
| FM-484 | orchestration | B35's dispatched agent ran 5+ hours and had not written receipts/B35.json, which the coordinator (per hc-FM-482, filed minutes earlier in the same session) treated as strong evidence of a stall and took over its verification work directly. Two SendMessage status-check nudges went unanswered with no completion notification for several minutes. The coordinator then called TaskStop on the agent. The killed agent's final output (delivered via the stop notification itself) revealed it had NOT been stalled at all: it had independently discovered the exact same PR#735 gaming defect the coordinator found (rename-and-wrap instead of real decomposition), rejected that path itself, and was mid-way through a THIRD build iteration (r5, forked from r4) that had already found and fixed 4 genuine dropped-local bareword-execution bugs (a real, separate defect class: lines like 'checkout_leak_detected="false" leak_commits=\'[]\' history_leak_json' losing their 'local' keyword during extraction, causing the trailing bareword to be executed as a command, exit 127) -- confirmed via an exhaustive static cross-check against every 'local' declaration in the base file. It was running the full acceptance suite on r5 in a background job at the moment it was killed. Recovery: the r5 worktree/branch were still on disk (git worktrees are not torn down by TaskStop), one further uncommitted fix from the killed agent's last edit was verified (bash -n clean, correct semantics) and committed by the coordinator, and the actual decomposition was confirmed genuine this time (max function 375 lines, function count 235->253, +18 not +1). No work was permanently lost only because cli-dispatch's worktree-per-lane model keeps uncommitted/committed work on a persistent branch rather than in the killed process's own memory. SIGNATURE: 'long runtime + missing expected artifact + unanswered SendMessage' is NOT sufficient evidence of a stall when the task involves multi-round iteration on a genuinely hard problem (here: a 13,900-line file requiring careful mechanical verification) -- a receipt file only gets written at the END of a lane's own workflow, so its absence is equally consistent with 'still iterating on round 3 of a real fix' as with 'stuck doing nothing'. An unanswered SendMessage during active tool-calling is not itself informative; the agent processes messages between its own tool rounds, not instantly. Recommendation: before TaskStop-ing a long-running agent based on artifact-absence, check git worktree/branch state for that leaf FIRST (as done immediately after, too late) -- if fresh commits exist with recent timestamps, the agent is actively iterating and should not be killed; only stop agents whose worktree/branch state is also stale relative to the runtime. TaskStop is irreversible for in-memory reasoning state even when git state survives, so escalate to it only after confirming via git log --since=<nudge-time> that no recent commits exist. [recurrence_of: none] | Coordinator killed a genuinely-active, productive agent after misdiagnosing it as stalled, based on runtime length and receipt-file absence alone | Judgment |
| FM-485 | validation-authenticity | B43 (homelab-config#187, container-registry HA design): run-validation.sh had each writer cross-read only its OWN digest through the other replica pod, but CONCURRENCY-RESULT.md's summary line asserted 'each replica's cross-read of the OTHER writer's blob matched' -- a claim the per-writer test didn't literally back (each of the two independent 1-directional checks together only implied it, not exercised it as a joint fact). Caught by an advisor review before a cross-harness (Codex) reviewer read it; fixed by adding a post-join consolidated check that fetches both digests from both replica pods (all 4 combinations) so the artifact's claim matches exactly what ran. Check: when a validation artifact makes a claim about joint/cross state (X observed via Y, for all X,Y pairs), verify the script actually iterates the full combination it claims, not just N independent single-direction checks that happen to add up to the same English sentence. [recurrence_of: none] | A self-authored validation artifact's PASS sentence claimed a stronger property than the script actually exercised | Judgment |
| FM-486 | Unclassified | clearwatch PR#4870 (fixing #4869's silent kubeconfig fallback) added 'kubectl config current-context 2>/dev/null' to detect a missing current-context field, but the 2>/dev/null also swallows kubectl's real parse error when the decoded kubeconfig is syntactically invalid YAML -- both cases get the same generic 'declares no current-context' message. Still fails loud (exit 1), not a functional regression, but it's the same wrong-cause-diagnosis failure class the PR exists to eliminate. No test exercises genuinely-malformed YAML, only valid-YAML-missing-field. Fix: capture stderr separately, branch the message on whether kubectl itself errored (parse failure) vs succeeded with an empty context string. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #4870) | CI fail-loud fix under 2>/dev/null can suppress the real parse error and report a generic wrong-cause message | Judgment |
| FM-487 | Unclassified | Campaign leaf B1 (clearwatch PR#4870) was dispatched for review AND a follow-up fix, both without reading gates/B1.md past its opening scope section. The file's closing lines (65-70) carry six ABANDON markers stating the whole clearwatch track is PARKED pending founder WAIT Q5, referenced from CAMPAIGN.md's own 'clearwatch + engram-go WAIT for estate phase' ruling and LEAVES.tsv. A separately-dispatched B2 agent caught this correctly (it read the gate file in full per its own instructions and refused to proceed), which is what surfaced the block after the fact -- the coordinator's own review dispatch and PR comment on #4870, plus a fix-forward agent that got partway through editing test files before being stopped, had already happened by then. Fix: before dispatching ANY leaf, grep the gate file for 'ABANDON' (or read it end-to-end) as a mandatory pre-dispatch step, not something left to the dispatched agent's own diligence to catch. [recurrence_of: none] | Coordinator dispatched a leaf whose own gate file already carried ABANDON/PARKED markers, because it only read the file's first ~30 lines before dispatching | Judgment |
| FM-488 | Unclassified | While stopping a leaf's fix-forward agent (a3782e86ad6e6ff71, B1/clearwatch), the coordinator also called TaskStop on a5b30e4e08ec0c00e purely because it appeared adjacent in the most recent ListAgents output and was assumed to be that same agent's codex-rescue sub-dispatch. TaskStop's own return message immediately revealed the true command name -- 'Cross-harness review of recall floor change' -- which was actually part of B10-unblock, a SEPARATE, founder-authorized lane with no relationship to B1 at all. No commits were lost (verified: PR#177's head was unchanged, the killed task was read-only review, not a push), but the parent B10-unblock agent was left to wait indefinitely on a review result that would never arrive until the coordinator caught its own mistake and messaged it to re-dispatch. This is the exact hc-FM-484 lesson (verify an agent's actual identity/state before TaskStop) recurring in the very same session it was first written down, this time via mis-association rather than mis-diagnosed staleness. Fix: TaskStop calls must name a task_id read from that task's OWN dispatch record (the agentId returned when it was launched, or its description string as printed by ListAgents/the task-notification that names it), never inferred from adjacency in an unrelated tool call's output. [recurrence_of: c-018] | Coordinator TaskStop'd an agent identified only by its position in a mental list, not by reading what the tool's own return value said it was, killing unrelated founder-authorized work | Judgment |
| FM-489 | Portability | fleet PR#738 (B36 fuzz leaf) found this via differential fuzzing: echo "$(nohup sleep 300 &)" and x=$(nohup y) both score background=false against dispatch/cli-dispatch even though bash evaluates $()/backtick substitutions immediately regardless of position or double-quoting. A false negative in the exact function backing the founder's fleet#462 exit-45 refusal ruling -- changes the refusal decision for a genuinely dangerous input. Filed as fleet#739, not fixed in PR#738 (recursing into substitutions found anywhere, including inside double quotes, is a larger and riskier change than that leaf's fuzz-and-fix scope; recommended as its own leaf). [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #739) | bg_scan_is_background only recurses into $()/backtick command substitution at command-word start, so a substitution invoking nohup/setsid anywhere else (an argument, inside double quotes, on an assignment RHS) evades the background-refusal scan entirely. | Judgment |
| FM-490 | Portability | fleet PR#738 (B36 fuzz leaf) differential fuzz corpus class amp-heredoc (indices 108-111): 'cat <<EOF' with a body line containing '&' scores background=true even though the & is heredoc data fed to cat, never executed as shell syntax. A false positive (over-refusal, the safe direction) rather than a hole in the refusal ruling. Filed as fleet#740, not fixed in PR#738 -- closing it needs multi-line heredoc-delimiter-aware parsing (including whether the delimiter was quoted, which changes whether the body itself gets substitution), out of scope for that leaf's narrow fuzz-and-fix budget. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #740) | bg_scan_is_background scans a here-doc body as ordinary unquoted shell text, so a literal & / nohup / setsid appearing only in the here-doc's DATA is misread as live syntax and the whole command is scored background=true. | Judgment |
| FM-491 | Portability | fleet PR#738 (B36 fuzz leaf), differential fuzz corpus class amp-redirect, indices 118-119: 'cmd &>file' and 'cmd &>>file' scored background=true against dispatch/cli-dispatch at main before the fix. Fixed in the same PR: treat & followed by > the same as & preceded by > (consume as part of the redirect, do not treat as the backgrounding operator). Regression test added in dispatch/test/background-refusal.bats; a differential mutant reverting this exact fix is checked into dispatch/fuzz/bg-scan/mutant_fn.sh to prove the new fuzz harness (dispatch/fuzz/bg-scan/) detects a regression of it. [recurrence_of: none] | bg_scan_is_background's own docstring says &&, >&, and N>& redirects are excluded from the backgrounding operator, but the leading-ampersand &> / &>> form (bash's combined stdout+stderr redirect) was missing from the exclusion list, so it scored background=true (false positive / over-refusal). | Judgment |
| FM-492 | Install / Config Layout Integrity | cli-dispatch's audition_index_verify (dispatch/cli-dispatch, fleet) walked its hash-chained obligation-index journal with a plain 'while IFS= read -r line; do ... done <"$idx"'. bash's 'read' returns non-zero for a final line lacking a trailing newline, so the loop BODY NEVER RUNS for it. The sole writer (_audition_journal_append_locked) does 'printf %s\n "$line" >>"$idx"' then separately updates a head pointer file -- so a process killed between those two steps (or mid-printf) leaves exactly an unterminated trailing line. Verification's own entry count then silently stopped one short, which matched the (also-unwritten) head pointer, so index, head, and the off-repo tamper-evidence anchor all agreed with each other: the torn write was completely invisible, not even reported as a discrepancy, despite 4+ prior hardening rounds (C-D3, AD-1..AD-5) explicitly targeting concurrent-write/partial-write classes on this exact file. Fix (fleet PR#741, B37): 'while IFS= read -r line || [[ -n "$line" ]]; do' forces the partial remainder through the loop body once more, where it fails the pre-existing 'jq -e type==object' check and gets the same die-30 refusal an interior corrupt line already receives. General lesson: any append-only/journal verifier that reads its file with a bare 'while read' (not 'while read || [[ -n $line ]]') will silently treat an interrupted trailing write as if it never happened -- check every such reader in a codebase for the same idiom gap, not just the one instance found. [recurrence_of: none] | Append-only journal verification via 'while read' silently drops a torn trailing line, hiding a crash-mid-append | Judgment |
| FM-493 | Unclassified | Dispatching a cross-harness review for B18 (duramind design doc), the coordinator wrote the review prompt with a literal '$(cat /path/to/file)' expecting the file contents to be substituted, since this pattern works when passed through Bash. Agent/Task prompts are plain strings handed directly to the subagent, not run through a shell, so the reviewer received the literal unexpanded string and correctly returned a FAIL citing 'document absent' rather than fabricating a review. Caught by the reviewing agent itself, not by the coordinator noticing before dispatch. Cost: one wasted dispatch round (28k tokens, ~44s). Fix: when a prompt needs a file's contents inlined, Read the file first and paste its actual text into the prompt string — never rely on shell-style command substitution inside a prompt parameter. [recurrence_of: none] | Coordinator embedded $(cat file) inside an Agent-tool prompt string expecting shell expansion, but Agent prompts are not executed through a shell | Judgment |
| FM-494 | Unclassified | B37 (fleet cli-dispatch audition-ledger hardening, PR#741) agent stopped mid-task saying 'I'll stop polling now and wait for the monitor's notification that all PR checks have reached a terminal state' -- but nothing external resumes a stopped subagent, and worse, the checks were not merely pending: 4 of them (Secret scan, orchestration, bats/orch, check-gaming/shard-0) had ALREADY reached a terminal FAILURE state at the moment the agent stopped, which a single gh pr checks call would have shown. This compounds the known hc-FM-259/270 'waiting for a background monitor' family with a second failure: the agent didn't even check whether there was something ALREADY actionable before deciding to wait. Coordinator caught it via a routine ListAgents+gh pr view check (not a special diagnostic), fetched the actual failing job URLs, and resumed the agent with the concrete failure data inline rather than a generic 'check your PR' nudge. Fix direction: dispatch briefs for any lane whose acceptance involves CI should say explicitly 'before saying you are waiting for CI, first check gh pr checks — if any check has already failed, that is not something to wait out, it is something to fix now.' [recurrence_of: c-027] | Dispatched agent stopped with 'I'll wait for the monitor's notification' when its own PR had real CI failures, not merely pending checks | Judgment |
| FM-495 | Cross-harness dispatch sandbox / trust | During B10 (hindsight PR#177 recall-floor unblock), a codex-rescue cross-harness review dispatch failed twice at the sandbox level trying to create scratch dirs under /tmp ('Read-only file system'), before succeeding on retry via /dev/shm. The FIRST failed run still printed a full multi-step review with a specific verdict-shaped conclusion ('Disqualifying reasons: ... required live causal reproduction remains unverified ...') under a banner explicitly saying not to trust it as delivered work -- the banner was the only signal distinguishing a real verdict from tooling wreckage; the prose alone read as a legitimate FAIL. The coordinator correctly discarded that run rather than posting it as review evidence, and re-dispatched with explicit /dev/shm guidance, which succeeded and produced a verdict corroborated by the coordinator's own independent measurement. Lesson: a subagent's own narrative conclusion is not evidence of a completed run; check for an explicit run-level failure marker before trusting or posting any dispatched review's verdict, especially when the sandbox's writable paths are non-obvious (/tmp read-only, /dev/shm and /var/tmp workable). [recurrence_of: none] | A codex-rescue dispatch's sandbox rejects /tmp as read-only, and a run that hits this can still emit a coherent-looking conclusion under a 'COMMAND FAILURE -- DELEGATE TOOLING DID NOT COMPLETE' banner | Judgment |
| FM-496 | Ephemeral Resource Leakage / Host Exhaustion | factvault#306 (dep bump fixing govulncheck CVEs for #305) merged 2026-08-18T11:11:53Z, but issue #305 stayed OPEN. Campaign leaf B20 was planned 2026-08-24/25 to 'bump dependencies to eliminate transitive CVEs' for #305, assuming the work was still outstanding. Caught before any wasted bump/PR by running the live scan and diffing the worktree before writing code, rather than trusting the issue's OPEN state as a signal that work remained. Lesson: an issue's open/closed state is not reliable evidence of whether its fix has landed -- verify against the artifact (a live scan, a diff, a test) before scoping build work off issue metadata. | FM-497 | test fixture invariant | B37 (fleet PR#741, dispatch/cli-dispatch audition_index_verify hardening, distinct from hc-FM-492 which is the underlying read-loop bug itself): the corrupt fixture's audition-index.head said 'seq=3 <hash-of-full-third-line>' -- byte-identical to the clean fixture's head file -- while audition-index.jsonl's third line was deliberately truncated to simulate a crash mid-append. The PR's own stated invariant (_audition_journal_append_locked writes the head pointer only AFTER the journal append line lands) means a real crash mid-append-3 cannot advance head past seq=2; as constructed the fixture instead advanced head to match the untruncated clean case, so it failed even against the PRE-fix code via an unrelated head/count mismatch, not via the specific read-loop bug (silent drop of an unterminated final line) the PR claimed to isolate. Caught by independent cross-harness (Codex) review, not by the author. Fix: set head to the last value that would actually survive the simulated crash (here, seq=2 with the hash of the complete second line, recoverable from the next line's own 'prev' field in the hash chain) and verify BOTH directions explicitly -- old code silently accepts the corrupted fixture, new code refuses via the exact named check -- rather than only checking that the fixed code refuses. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #741) | A test fixture's own metadata must be re-derived to satisfy the invariant it fakes, not copied from the clean control | Judgment |
| FM-498 | Unclassified | cli-dispatch's C3 pre-provision background-command scanner (bg_scan_is_background, dispatch/cli-dispatch) promises refusal before provisioning for any --check/--check-script string that backgrounds. Confirmed two real bypasses on fleet PR#738's HEAD (1b4ca3d), independently reproduced by the coordinator (not just cited from the reviewer): (1) an ampersand inside a double-quoted $(...) command substitution, e.g. echo "$(nohup sleep 300 &)", is invisible to the scanner because the in_dq branch at cli-dispatch:4700 skips quoted content char-by-char without checking for background markers inside it. (2) bash's coproc keyword is a direct async primitive with zero corpus coverage (225-entry corpus, gen_corpus.py:143's class list omits it) and is not classified as background either -- reproduced a real surviving orphaned child process outliving its parent shell. Both return the scanner's 'foreground' classification (exit 1) where the correct classification is background (exit 0), which is the exact inverse of the fail-closed behavior C3 exists to guarantee. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #738) | bg_scan_is_background misses quoted-cmdsubst and coproc background primitives | Judgment |
| FM-499 | Unclassified | gates/B37.md's G5 evidence text was drafted stating a follow-up gap 'Filed as fleet#743' before gh issue create was ever run for it -- the number was picked/assumed rather than obtained from an actual API call. Caught on re-verification when fleet#743 turned out to already belong to an unrelated finding filed later in the same session (a B36 coproc gap), an unrelated collision that surfaced the gap rather than a systematic check catching it. Fix: filed for real as fleet#744, corrected the evidence text. General rule: a gate's EVIDENCE line asserting 'Filed as #N' must quote the number FROM a completed gh issue create call's own output, never write the sentence before running the command -- same class as the standing 'claims are not evidence' rule, applied to the coordinator's own gate-authoring, not just to relayed subagent claims. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #741) | Gate evidence claimed an issue was filed before it actually existed | Judgment |
| FM-500 | Unclassified | Running node gate-check.mjs --status with no file args (the documented default: GATES.md + all of gates/*.md, 174 files in this campaign) reported B42.md, B47.md, and R9.md as having 6, 2, and 4 unmet gates respectively. Re-running gate-check.mjs --status against EACH of those files individually, seconds later, with no file changes in between, reported ALL MET for all three. This was caught only because the coordinator was about to dispatch real leaf-scope work against B42 and R9 based on the sweep's false UNMET counts -- both leaves were already fully done. Root cause not yet isolated (candidates: the sweep's serial execution across 174 files' CHECK commands running long enough to interact with gate-check's own default 120s per-check timeout in a way that manifests as a false failure only in the full-sweep context; some form of shared-state or concurrency artifact when many files are checked in one invocation vs one). Rule going forward: NEVER treat the full default sweep's per-file unmet count as ground truth for whether a specific leaf needs more work -- always re-run gate-check.mjs --status <that one file> in isolation before dispatching work or reporting a leaf incomplete. The isolated, single-file result is the trustworthy one; the aggregate sweep is useful only as a first-pass triage signal to decide WHICH files to re-check individually, never as the final word on any one file's state. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #none) | gate-check.mjs full-sweep --status is unreliable per-file; only isolated single-file runs are trustworthy | Judgment |
| FM-501 | Unclassified | homelab-kubernetes PR#33 (leaf B48, plan parallel-2026-08-24) fixed a fail-open bug where a hardcoded 2-list test partition let a new cluster-requiring test get globbed into the wrong CI job and SKIP there non-fatally. The fix replaced the hardcoded lists with a '# requires: cluster' marker read via a single anchored, case-sensitive grep; anything not matching that EXACT string (a typo missing the 's', wrong case, a marker indented with leading whitespace) fell through to an unconditional else-branch default of marker=hosted -- recreating the identical fail-open failure mode one layer down, now triggered by a marker typo instead of a forgotten list entry. Caught by a dispatched cross-harness review answering the gate's own explicit question ('what happens to a test with a misspelled marker'), independently confirmed by the coordinator reading the actual grep pattern and matching it against the concrete misspelling/case/indentation cases by hand. SIGNATURE: replacing an enumerated list with a marker/tag-based classifier does not, by itself, close a fail-open hole -- if unmatched input silently falls to a default branch rather than refusing loudly, the fail-open property has just moved from 'is this thing in my list' to 'did I match the marker exactly,' and a typo is a more common real-world failure than an omitted list entry. Any tag/marker-driven selector meant to close a fail-open gap must have NO silent default: unmatched or malformed input must cause a loud, blocking failure that names the offending item, not a fallback classification. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #33) | A marker-based test-selector fix silently reintroduced its own fail-open class via a strict-match-or-default classifier | Judgment |
| FM-502 | Unclassified | R12 (plan parallel-2026-08-24, learning-loop null-arm probe, H12) copied the learning-ab-2 harness into a scratch dir and ran it via an unpinned require() of the live orchestration/src/ tree. The original two retained ties this probe was meant to compare against were measured 2026-07-20 at commit 5fbab45, BEFORE the D1 host-executed-acceptance gate existed. The unpinned require() picked up creation-loop.js as it exists today (commit 7efc1f1/5a0a157, 2026-08-17) -- roughly a month of unrelated code drift later, now running through a differently-behaving driver (the D1 gate). The probe's own honest FINDINGS.md correctly flagged this: the null-arm spread computed today (0) cannot be compared apples-to-apples against the original ties' margins, even though the raw numbers happen to line up, because the instrument itself changed between the two measurements. SIGNATURE: any probe designed to compare against previously-recorded evidence must pin the exact commit/version of the thing being re-run, not just copy it into a scratch dir and require() the live tree -- 'copy the harness out so it doesn't touch the real repo' (an isolation concern) is a different requirement from 'pin the harness to the SAME version the original measurement used' (a comparability concern), and satisfying the first does not satisfy the second. A scratch-dir copy of unpinned live code is exactly as vulnerable to drift as running in place would have been. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #none) | A research probe's unpinned require() silently re-ran against a month-drifted harness version, invalidating comparison to prior pinned evidence | Judgment |
| FM-503 | Unclassified | Fixing fleet#740 (heredoc false-positive in bg_scan_is_background) was dispatched to grok-build:grok-delegate first -- it ran 619s, hit the harness's own foreground time window, was moved to background, and reported 'I'll report the result once notified' even though nothing external resumes a stopped Agent-tool subagent (the standing waiting-for-a-monitor anti-pattern, hc-FM-259/270 etc). Checked directly: zero PR, zero commits, zero issue comments -- no progress at all. Redispatched to codex:codex-rescue with an explicit anti-detach instruction ('work incrementally, commit as you go'); it instead immediately kicked off an ASYNC INNER Codex task (task-mt9b02bo-z13jtx) and returned after 21s reporting 'started in background, check /codex:status <id>' -- a slash command not invocable as a tool from the coordinator, and the codex-rescue subagent itself had already completed (no live children per ListAgents), so nothing tracks that inner task's completion either. Checked directly again: still zero PR, zero branch, zero comment. Two DIFFERENT harnesses failed the identical task via two DIFFERENT detachment mechanisms (foreground-window backgrounding vs. self-initiated async sub-dispatch) before any real progress existed -- correctly triggering the standing 2-same-class-failure loop cap. Fix: switched method to general-purpose (no external harness indirection), with explicit synchronous-work-only instructions and 'do not background/detach, commit incrementally.' General lesson: 'started in the background, will report later' from a dispatched agent is not evidence of eventual completion -- verify against the real external artifact (PR/commit/comment) immediately, in both directions (does the harness's own foreground/background threshold apply, and did the harness itself spawn a further async job it does not supervise) before assuming it merely needs more wall-clock time. [recurrence_of: c-027] (GitHub #740) | Two different harnesses failed the same task via two different mechanisms before the loop cap forced a method change | Judgment |

| FM-505 | Unclassified | aifleet controller TRUSTED_PROXY_CIDR was pinned to the pod CIDRs of the two nodes Traefik happened to run on. Traefik rescheduled; the allowlist was never updated; watcherIdentityFromRequest stopped trusting the forwarded client-cert header and every watcher POST /status got 401 for 13 days. Neither side alarmed: the controller logs nothing on a rejected watcher POST and the watcher only WARNs, so total heartbeat loss across all 3 GPU hosts was found only by accident when a new dashboard read /inventory. Lesson: an allowlist keyed to a schedulable pod's current node is a time bomb; derive it dynamically or alarm on zero-valid-reports-for-N-minutes. A guard that fails closed still needs a liveness alarm, because failing closed silently is indistinguishable from working. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #636) | Hand-maintained node-pinned CIDR allowlist goes stale on pod reschedule, silently 401ing every client | Judgment |
| FM-506 | Unclassified | B44 (homelab-config#148, container-registry chown prep) receipt claimed proxmox.petersimmons.com and trunas.petersimmons.com resolve to the same underlying TrueNAS dataset (zp1/k8s-data), reached via different host aliases -- based on a TrueNAS RPC metadata field, never on actually resolving both hostnames or SSHing to both. False: dig+ssh hostname confirms proxmox.petersimmons.com is host 'pve' (192.168.0.100, a Proxmox hypervisor with its own local zp3 ZFS pool) and trunas.petersimmons.com is host 'truenas' (192.168.0.189), two entirely different machines. The live registry PV is actually served from Proxmox's own zp3 pool via a plain Linux NFS export, never touching TrueNAS at all. The entire G2/G3 backup+rehearsal (TrueNAS zp1/scratch-b44, hourly snapshot task) rehearsed against the WRONG storage backend. A first cross-harness review (Grok) caught the path mismatch as a real, independently-reasoned finding and correctly flagged it PASS-WITH-CONDITIONS rather than accepting the receipt's framing -- but even that review did not push far enough to check whether the ACTUAL backend (zp3) has any backup mechanism at all; the coordinator's own follow-up SSH check found zero snapshots and no snapshot cron/timer on zp3, meaning there is currently NO verified restorable backup for this live data. General lesson: a receipt asserting two distinct hostnames/paths are 'actually the same resource' is exactly the kind of claim that needs independent resolution (DNS + hostname, not a metadata field from one side) before any rehearsal built on it is trusted -- and a cross-harness review that catches a mismatch should also be asked to verify the corrected target has the safety properties (backup existence) the original claim assumed, not just that the technical mechanism (chown honoring NFS semantics) would work. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #148) | A lane's receipt self-certified two hostnames as 'the same dataset via different aliases' without ever resolving/comparing them, and the false claim survived a full rehearsal + one cross-harness review pass undetected | Judgment |

| FM-508 | Unclassified | Memory-audit S1.2 (2026-08-26) built a MEMORY.md reachability check by tracing markdown [](...)  links only, 2 hops from the index. 28 of 255 payload files came back unreachable by that method, but MEMORY.md's own text documents a second pointer path -- repo-level CLAUDE.md files pointing BACK to payload files by prose filename mention, not markdown link syntax -- which the link-graph parser cannot see. Several of the 28 orphans matched that exact pattern (project-agentgateway.md, project_k8s_source_of_truth.md, reference-embed-hardware-topology.md) and were confirmed reachable via that second path on manual inspection, so the 28-file orphan count is only an upper bound, not a confirmed targeting gap. General shape: any index/pointer-coverage tool that walks one link syntax will overcount orphans when a second, out-of-band pointer convention (prose mention in a sibling file/repo) also confers reachability. Fix for next run: enumerate all pointer conventions actually in use (grep prose filename mentions across repo CLAUDE.md files, not just MEMORY.md's own markdown links) before computing an orphan count. [recurrence_of: none] | Index-coverage link-graph audits miss cross-file prose pointers, understating orphan risk | Judgment |

| FM-511 | Deploy / Verify Discipline | homelab-config#202 was fixed by a PR in homelab-kubernetes (#35). GitHub's closing keywords (Closes/Fixes/Resolves #N) only work within the same repository as the PR -- there is no cross-repo auto-close mechanism, even when the PR body explicitly says 'Closes owner/repo#N'. The issue stayed open after merge with no signal anything was wrong, until a coordinator explicitly cross-checked issue state against the merge. Any cross-repo fix (a shared dependency, a common controller/helm-chart repo fixing multiple downstream consumers' issues) needs an explicit manual gh issue close/comment step -- it will never happen automatically. [recurrence_of: none] | A fix landing in a different repo than its issue never auto-closes that issue, and looks silently forgotten if nobody checks | Judgment |
| FM-512 | Deploy / Verify Discipline | fleet PR#749 (fleet#744 follow-up): a fix for 'function returns 1 instead of 0 on a masked relay-write failure' added a test that ran the function under set -e and asserted the shell aborted (SURVIVED never printed). Independent review (round 3) found this test does NOT discriminate the bug it claims to lock: the buggy prior version had the shape 'if [[ relay-set ]]; then printf ...; fi; return 0' -- under set -e, a failing printf inside the if-body aborts the whole function via errexit BEFORE the buggy unconditional 'return 0' line is ever reached, so the buggy version aborts too, identically to the fixed version. Both implementations produce the same observable behavior (abort, no SURVIVED) under set -e, even though their actual return-status contract differs. The bug is visible only in the function's OWN exit status observed under set +e (assign to a variable or check $? directly) -- reproduced empirically: reconstructing the exact buggy body and running it under set +e gives RC=0 (masked), while the fixed body gives RC=1 (propagated), proving the set +e assertion is discriminating where the set -e one was not. General lesson: when a fix's whole point is a function's own return-status contract, assert that status directly under set +e; wrapping the call in a set -e script and checking whether the SCRIPT aborted is testing something else (whether errexit fires on SOME nonzero exit somewhere in the chain), which can pass identically on both the correct and the still-broken implementation whenever the differing code path sits after a point where the buggy path already fails hard for an unrelated reason. [recurrence_of: none] | A regression test that asserts under set -e can pass on both the buggy AND the fixed implementation, if errexit aborts before the line that differs between them ever runs | Judgment |

| FM-515 | Container Runtime — Design | fleet PR #747 round-3 review (task review-747-r3, Codex): every dispatch-lane process lives in a kernel cgroup scope (cli-dispatch-lane-<task>.scope, confirmed via /proc/<pid>/cgroup), which fleet#207's lane_ancestor_record checks unconditionally and cannot be evaded by changing cwd (a fresh /tmp git clone does not help -- verified empirically, matching round-2's identical 9/59-shaped failure). dispatch/test/background-refusal.bats invokes 'cli-dispatch start' as part of ~52 of its 59 fixtures, so those tests are refused with the correct, by-design fleet#207 process-lineage refusal whenever the invoking bats process is itself a dispatch-lane descendant -- structurally, regardless of the reviewer's chosen working directory. This is a permanent channel/target mismatch, not a one-off environment bug: any independent review dispatched via 'cli-dispatch start' (the only sanctioned channel for Codex/Grok) can verify everything about this PR except the full bats gate. Worked around this round by having the coordinator (not lane-scoped) and a non-lane grok-build delegate independently clone+run the suite outside any lane (20/20 clean runs of the specific failing-looking test, 59/59 and 58/59 -- the one Grok failure was a sandbox pkill EPERM on an unrelated fleet#462 reaping test, not a regression). No merge decision should treat a lane-dispatched reviewer's FAIL-due-to-bats-blocked as a code-correctness finding; the review-post artifact must say so explicitly, and a human/founder call is needed on whether ground-truth-run evidence from a non-reviewer source can substitute for the missing lane-capable gate. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #747) | A cli-dispatch-launched reviewer cannot certify a PR whose bats suite itself invokes cli-dispatch start | Judgment |
| FM-516 | Judgment | On fleet PR#747, an independent review round FAILed for a purely environmental reason (fleet#207 cgroup lineage refusal blocking the bats gate), zero real code defect found. The coordinator independently re-ran the gates itself in a clean clone, confirmed clean, then posted a PASS verdict via cli-dispatch review-post --harness claude and was about to merge -- self-certification, since Claude authored the fix via its own dispatched builders. Caught before merging by explicit self-check against the standing 'author does not certify its own work' rule, not by any tooling gate. Correct remedy: treat the coordinator's own verification as informational context only and dispatch one more genuinely independent (non-Claude) review with that context. Verifying a FAIL was environmental is legitimate coordinator work; the closing PASS verdict must still come from a non-author harness. [recurrence_of: none] | A coordinator that authored a fix must not post its own harness's review verdict, even to confirm a FAIL was environmental | Judgment |
| FM-517 | Container Runtime — Operational | aifleet watcher/internal/docker.go built container Env by injecting HF_HOME=/mnt/ai-models/hf-cache then appending spec.EnvVars (which could also set HF_HOME), assuming Docker/libc resolves the later entry. It doesn't -- the container's process saw the FIRST HF_HOME, silently discarding the operator's override, crashlooping Nemotron 3.5 Lightning on oblivion under HF_HUB_OFFLINE. Fixed with a dedupeEnvVarsLastWins() pass before the Env list reaches Docker's container-create body; regression test confirmed red without the fix. Check: any code building a container Env list from multiple sources (defaults + operator overrides) must dedupe by key before passing to Docker -- appending later does not imply precedence. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #642) | Docker container Env list with duplicate keys resolves to the FIRST entry via getenv(), not the last -- appending an operator override after a watcher-injected default does not shadow it. | Judgment |
| FM-518 | Container Runtime — Operational | A fat training image baked Nemotron 3.5 Lightning's checkpoint at /opt/hf-cache/models--..., and the GPUHost CRD set HF_HOME=/opt/hf-cache expecting vLLM to find it there. It crashlooped with LocalEntryNotFoundError under HF_HUB_OFFLINE even though the model was on disk, because huggingface_hub.constants.HF_HUB_CACHE derives to $HF_HOME/hub -- i.e. it looked at /opt/hf-cache/hub/models--..., which didn't exist. Fixed by setting HF_HUB_CACHE directly instead of HF_HOME, bypassing the derived-path convention. Check: when pointing an offline HF cache at a custom baked-in directory, verify against huggingface_hub.constants.HF_HUB_CACHE (or just set that var directly), not HF_HOME -- the two are not interchangeable despite HF_HOME being the more commonly documented knob. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #473) | huggingface_hub resolves its actual cache directory as $HF_HOME/hub (the HF_HUB_CACHE constant), not $HF_HOME itself -- setting HF_HOME to point at a pre-baked cache directory silently looks one level too deep. | Judgment |
| FM-519 | Container Runtime — Operational | On precision, docker build / docker buildx build against the default builder failed immediately, 3 times in a row (including after killing the stuck client each time), with the daemon logging a session healthcheck failure at connect time. The client itself did not error out -- it hung indefinitely with zero build progress (docker buildx du showed 0 bytes transferred over several minutes). Wrong initial hypothesis: suspected the ai-fleet-watcher's docker-socket-proxy sidecar, ruled out via docker inspect showing it is bound to a separate compose network, not the host socket a direct SSH docker build uses. Root cause of the stuck default-builder session was never identified (would require a dockerd restart to inspect further, out of scope on a host running live production inference). Fix: docker buildx create --name NEWNAME --driver docker-container --use, then build against that builder explicitly. Confirmed working across two separate builds the same day. Check: do not assume a docker-adjacent sidecar is implicated just because it exists nearby -- verify the actual network/socket path before spending time on that hypothesis. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #644) | A host's default docker buildx builder can get stuck in a dead session state ('only one connection allowed', healthcheck fails fatally) that persists across killing the client process and retrying -- the fix is a fresh named builder, not diagnosing or restarting the daemon. | Judgment |

| FM-521 | Unclassified | fleet main: bats CI jobs use path-filtering so commits touching only unrelated files skip the 'bats -- dispatch/test shard' step entirely, but the job's overall conclusion still reports success. This masked two real, pre-existing, unrelated bugs (a stale token-idiom-lint allowlist entry, broken run-suite path resolution) for an unknown period, since nothing had recently touched dispatch/ to trigger a genuine run. PR#783 (an unrelated exit-code fix) was the first commit in a while to touch dispatch/, triggered a real run, and its own CI failures were initially and reasonably suspected as a regression from #783 before being root-caused via gh api .../jobs/<id>/steps (showing 'skipped' on the green baseline's actual test step) as pre-existing and unrelated. Fix direction: a path-filtered CI job whose gated step is skipped should report a neutral/skipped conclusion, never 'success' -- a green checkmark on main should mean tests ran and passed, not merely that nothing blocked the job. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #784) | Path-filtered CI reports a job as 'success' even when its actual test-running step was skipped, creating a false-green baseline | Judgment |
| FM-522 | Unclassified | fleet PR#765 (research#12 LV-NOTE-1 circuit-wiring adoption scorer), caught by required independent Codex review before merge, not by the PR's own tests. runners/circuit_wiring_adoption.py parsed every 'ok <name>' / 'FAIL <name>:' line from a subprocess's combined stdout+stderr, with a later FAIL overwriting an earlier ok in the result map, never checking proc.returncode and never restricting parsing to the private test runner's own canonical result block. Candidate resolver.py code executing inside run_tests.py -- including via an atexit handler firing after the real runner's output -- can emit matching ok lines and manufacture an adopted verdict regardless of what the private tests actually did. The PR's own tests (tests/test_circuit_wiring_adoption.py) only exercised ordinary fixtures; none proved candidate-controlled output could not alter a verdict. Same family as fleet-MDL-13/fleet-OPS-27 (eval scorers trusting unvalidated candidate-side signals) but a new instance/shape: a text-parsing integrity gap rather than an instrumentation-counting or budget gap. Fix direction: any scorer that runs candidate code and then parses that code's own output for a verdict must (1) validate the process exit code first, (2) parse results only from a delimited block the trusted runner itself emits, never raw combined output. [recurrence_of: none] (GitHub #765) | A behavioral eval scorer parses candidate-controlled combined stdout/stderr for ok/FAIL result lines without validating process returncode or restricting to a canonical result block, letting candidate code spoof its own passing verdict | Judgment |
| FM-523 | Identity vs. Routing | Codex and Grok companion plugins export CODEX_COMPANION_*/GROK_CC_* into every Claude Code session, so any harness detection by env-var prefix misclassifies Claude as Codex. Measured 2026-08-27 building fleet/hooks; detection must key on runner-injected markers (GROK_HOOK_EVENT), payload shape, or CLAUDECODE before generic prefixes. [recurrence_of: none] | Companion plugins leak CODEX_*/GROK_* env into Claude sessions | Judgment |
| FM-524 | Identity vs. Routing | Exporting GH_TOKEN=$(gh-app-token grok) before cli-dispatch start is not enough: gh_token_sync re-reads CLI_DISPATCH_GH_TOKEN_CACHE (default ~/.cache/gh-app-token/claude) and overwrites the exported token, so gate-753 refuses the Grok lane as psimmons-claude-worker[bot]. gh_token_remint also hardcodes claude. Fix: derive the cache/mint harness from --harness. Workaround: export CLI_DISPATCH_GH_TOKEN_CACHE=~/.cache/gh-app-token/grok. Observed twice 2026-08-27 (fleet#785 lanes). [recurrence_of: none] | cli-dispatch gh_token_sync silently replaces an exported Grok token with the Claude cache | Judgment |

A merge gate that cannot reach a second harness must refuse, not decide. Observed 2026-08-27 on fleet#785. | FM-528 | process: review-gate design | fleet PR #785 (cross-harness hooks H1-H7) took 8 adversarial review rounds (Codex r1-r4, Grok r1-r4), every one FAIL, every finding a new spelling of one class (shell execution prefixes, quoting, assignment builtins, redirect glue). Causes: (1) no cross-harness contact at plan time on the shell-grammar scope; (2) open-ended 'find a bypass' mandate against an accident-stopper cannot converge; (3) coordinator patched per finding through r3 instead of enumerating the class after the second same-class finding (own standing rule); (4) two reviewers on staggered heads made each fix invalidate the other lane's run, doubling rounds. Fix shape: scope list first, one enumeration round with disjoint mandates per harness producing a test corpus, build the normalizer table from the union, then one scoped review round on one head. 2026-08-27. [recurrence_of: none] | Regex-gate PRs get an enumeration round (both harnesses produce the in-scope spelling corpus, no verdict) BEFORE any adversarial review round; the in/out scope list is the spec, written first | Judgment |

Same 3 files vs the true origin/main were byte-identical. | FM-537 | gate-anchor-cannot-match | Same session as FM-530 (unsatisfiable /UNMET: 0/ anchor) and the coordinator already knew rg -c prints nothing on zero matches (used --include-zero in the pre-fix MEASUREMENT of the very same gate). gates/leaf-1.7.md G3 and G6 (fleet PR #788, leaf 1.7 attempt 1) still shipped without it, so a correct implementation was scored check-failed on 2 of 3 failing gates and the lane burned a retry with zero delta. Discriminator: any EXPECT that anchors on a zero count needs a CHECK that PRINTS zero; measuring pre-fix with one command and writing the gate with a different command lets the measurement lie about the gate. Mechanizable: gate-check.mjs (or a gates-file lint) should flag 'rg -c' without --include-zero when EXPECT can match 0. | FM-538 | panel-shrink-by-budget | fleet PR #788 fanout #5 (wf_ff04b7b3-95c, 2026-08-28): the security-regression lane agent ran cli-dispatch start --harness codex in a foreground shell; the start reached 'HEAD is now at a242b7b' (worktree created) and then the agent hit its turn budget, its final answer was forced, the shell died and took start with it before harness launch (no agent.log, no process, log frozen at 7 lines). The lane correctly refused to fabricate a verdict and returned dispatched:false, so the fanout reported panel_shrunk N=2 and a merge-gate decision was reached without the Codex axis -- same shape as FM-526 (ff-only refusal), different cause. Signature: any reviewer lane that launches a multi-minute external process inside its own turn budget is a coin flip on wall-clock. Mechanize: the fanout script should run cli-dispatch start in background mode (memory: cli-dispatch start needs background) and poll the lane's ledger/agent.log with a wall-clock deadline decoupled from the agent's turn count; a panel_shrunk result must block the decision, not annotate it (FM-286 shape). [recurrence_of: none] | review-fanout Codex lane killed by the lane agent's own turn budget after cli-dispatch start created the worktree; panel silently shrank to N=2 | 🤖 |

The grok token itself passed gate-753 when probed directly, cached and fresh. Root cause: the workflow's Grok lane prompt (~/.claude/workflows/review-fanout.js STEP 2) said nothing about the token, so reaching Grok depended on what the agent's shell inherited; fanouts #3-#6 reached Grok by luck, #7 did not. Mechanize further: the fanout script should treat panel_shrunk as a blocking decision input, not an annotation (FM-286), so a shrunk panel can never reach a merge-gate verdict. Fix applied to ~/.claude/workflows/review-fanout.js STEP 1c for both vendor lanes. Mechanize further: the fanout script must treat panel_shrunk as blocking (FM-286) -- three different causes all produced the same silent N=2, so the symptom is where the guard belongs.
